#+title: Beam Lisp, with reasons you can inspect
#+subtitle: One language for programs, contracts and the evidence behind them.
#+kicker: ENCHANT · FORMAL LOGIC LAB

* The big idea
#+begin_lede
Imagine writing a function and asking, in the same language: /what must be true before this runs, what can it return, and can this change break anything?/

The experiment gives Beam Lisp a common language of typed terms. Z3 and OxiZ can work on the same question. BL owns the meaning, and the answer brings its assumptions and limits with it.
#+end_lede

#+begin_stats
187 | shared questions | language values, native theories and machine arithmetic
12,151 | numeric SDK assertion executions | BEAM values, host ABI, Datom, managed requests and retained project behavior; other evidence has its own scope
2 | native backends | one portable BL question
46 | interface families | implemented fragments and future directions
#+end_stats

#+begin_principles
Write the meaning once | Maps, arithmetic and outcomes keep the same meaning when we change the solver.
Make answers inspectable | Show the claim, its assumptions, a counterexample when available, and the scope of the result.
Use preview to improve the language | Fix incoherent behavior before programs have to depend on it.
Keep a clear next step | A language with precise semantics and checked proofs is a realistic direction. Every stage needs its own evidence.
#+end_principles

#+begin_job :id story-contract :title "Explain why a change is safe" :codex "Run examples and inspect a diff" :claude "Read the implementation and suggest checks" :decision "Add a reusable contract and ask the same question of either backend." :screen story-desk
When I change a function, I want to know which promises still hold and see a concrete counterexample when one fails.
#+end_job

#+begin_graph :title "The three pieces"
node terms | Typed terms | The mathematics, as BL data | 0,0 | you
node meaning | Language meaning | Presence, outcomes and model bounds | 1,0 | station
node question | One question | Claim + assumptions + observations | 2,0 | door
node z3 | Z3 | Native C API | 1,1 | builder
node oxiz | OxiZ | Native Rust Context | 2,1 | builder
node answer | Inspectable answer | Verdict, witness, scope and provenance | 3,0 | you
edge terms -> meaning | describe values | solid
edge meaning -> question | give the claim its meaning | solid
edge question -> z3 | same typed question | solid
edge question -> oxiz | same typed question | solid
edge z3 -> answer | report evidence | dash
edge oxiz -> answer | report evidence | dash
#+end_graph

#+begin_honest :title "What exists today"
The term interface and the experiments are implemented. A new independent checker now accepts a small Boolean and linear integer proof format. The default compiler oracle still uses its existing path. Language-wide semantic changes, a verified checker and compiler integration remain work ahead. No result here has been promoted into compiler proof authority.
#+end_honest

** The implementation is moving
The autonomous goal now works directly on main. The first slice is executable: =formal/certify= searches for a proof object, and =formal/check-certificate= checks it afresh against the exact claim, assumptions, profile and scope. Both operations use BL terms and need no native solver.

The checker covers Boolean case splits and contradictions made from linear integer inequalities. It also checks strict integer bounds and exact rounding when dividing coefficients. Search is deliberately incomplete: a missing proof returns unknown. Other theories are refused by this proof system even when Z3 or OxiZ can decide them.

The first focused run passed 577 assertions: 511 for certificate checking, 42 for the existing term boundary and 24 for the public API. This includes rejecting altered statements, missing branches, forged terms and invalid arithmetic weights. These checks test the implementation; they are not a machine-checked proof of its soundness.

#+begin_note :title "What a checked result means here"
The claim follows from its listed assumptions in the supported term logic, according to this checker. Assumption feasibility, the connection to source semantics, the checker implementation and its BL/BEAM execution still need their own assurance. A checked term proof does not silently grant compiler authority.
#+end_note

Implementation: [[file:sdk-src/formal/certificate.bl][certificate checker]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/proof.bl][separate proof search]], [[file:FOUNDATION.md][foundation sequence and proof rules]], [[file:evidence/foundation/qualification.json][the first qualification receipt]]. Its live presentation passed 235 browser checks, and all four cells at that stage rechecked unchanged.

The next slice is also implemented: a small Int/Bool core with lexical binding, selective conditionals, sequencing, arithmetic and named errors. It has a concrete reference interpreter, a separate symbolic interpretation, and an elaborator from the real compiler's ANF. Its focused file passed 3,194 assertions, including 810 combinations of program and inputs comparing the two interpretations, and 42 comparisons with actual BL arithmetic/truthiness functions. These are finite implementation checks; core metatheory and compiler preservation remain unproved.

#+begin_principles
Describe the whole outcome | The core distinguishes returning, its value and the exact named error. Two different failures are not interchangeable.
Preserve evaluation order | Operands run left to right; the first failure ends the computation. An untaken branch cannot raise.
Keep limits outside the meaning | An interpreter step ceiling reports incomplete work, not a language exception.
Grow from a precise fragment | Int and Bool have no implicit casts here. Other BL values and effects need explicit semantic extensions.
#+end_principles

The grammar and rules are in [[file:CORE.md][the core specification]], with [[file:evidence/core/checks.txt][the actual core check log]].

Program contracts now build on that core. They carry parameter sorts, preconditions, postconditions, permitted errors and effect policy as closed BL data. Verification reconstructs the question from the definition, checks certificates where supported, and can ask either backend for replayable feasibility and counterexample witnesses. A separate proof can expose contradictory assumptions without a native solver.

The integrated run passed 4,575 assertions across ten files. The original 187 backend questions kept their prior verdicts. An OxiZ compound observation that stays opaque can now be computed from concrete inputs by BL's independent scalar evaluator; each displayed field retains its native availability and records whether it came from backend data or replay. This improves language-side observations without inventing a solver capability or a NaN payload.

See [[file:CONTRACTS.md][the program contract interface]], [[file:evidence/contracts/checks.txt][the integrated check log]] and [[file:evidence/contracts/qualification.json][its qualified source and backend receipts]]. Next: recursive/loop ranking obligations, broader effect and process contracts, checked translation, and foundation metatheory.

The first mathematical core checkpoint has a mechanically checked foundation too. Pinned Lean 4.34.1 checked 18 theorems covering typed model outcomes, deterministic evaluation, lexical binding, selected branches, first failure, signed division examples and well-founded ranking rules. Its printed audit reports no axiom dependencies for those theorems. This establishes facts about the Lean model, not that the BL implementation or compiler faithfully implements it.

Local binding names are now canonical: recompiling the same source no longer changes a contract identity just because the compiler picked fresh temporary names. The follow-up core/contract/API run passed 3,302 assertions.

Read [[file:metatheory/README.md][the exact mechanized scope]], [[file:metatheory/Core.lean][the checked model and proofs]], [[file:evidence/metatheory/qualification.json][the proof receipt]], and [[file:evidence/contracts/identity-checks.txt][the identity follow-up log]].

** Ordinary loops, with an unbounded argument
The next implementation accepts an ordinary BL =loop/recur= expression through the real compiler's ANF. A contract supplies a precondition, invariant, integer ranking vector and result condition. The tool checks initialization, normal evaluation on each reachable path, invariant preservation, strict rank decrease and the exit result. One decreasing counter handles a countdown; a lexicographic pair handles an inner counter that resets when an outer counter decreases.

These are symbolic arguments with no iteration bound. The separate reference executor has resource ceilings and reports incomplete work when it reaches one. A bad invariant or rank yields =obligation-refuted=: its countermodel may be unreachable in the actual execution. That does not establish nontermination of the program.

The focused loop/core/contract/API run passed 3,404 assertions, including 102 loop assertions. Six examples ran on both native backends with the expected results: three checked model arguments, a growing-loop rank failure, a loose invariant with an unreachable countermodel, and inconsistent inputs. An unused failing branch can carry an explicit inconsistency certificate. An author's =decreasing= assertion cannot replace a checked argument.

The loop checkpoint has twenty-two checked theorems. In addition to ranking orders, it defines an unbounded evaluator from a total controller and a well-founded back-edge relation, and proves that exit postconditions carry through that evaluation. Twenty theorem audits have empty axiom dependency lists. The two nonnegative integer ranking proofs use Lean's standard =propext=, =Classical.choice= and =Quot.sound= assumptions, which are listed in the receipt and returned rule metadata. The correspondence between the BL implementation and that mathematical model remains reviewed and unproved.

See [[file:LOOPS.md][the loop interface and its exact meaning]], [[file:evidence/loops/qualification.json][the scoped qualification]], [[file:evidence/loops/checks.txt][the focused checks]], [[file:evidence/loops/examples.json][the actual definitions, proof objects and backend observations]], and [[file:evidence/loops/model-qualification.json][the model and axiom ledger]].

** Let the optimizer propose; make the evidence decide
The next piece compares two exact core programs. It asks whether they return the same value or raise the same named error under the same precondition. A separate optimizer now proposes real rewrites, and the translation boundary reconstructs the comparison from both programs. Only an independently checked proof lets that gate return the changed core. Other answers keep the original.

This catches a subtle mistake: multiplying an expression by zero still evaluates that expression. If it raises, replacing the whole computation with zero changes the program. Our rewrite keeps that evaluation before returning zero. Equal conditional branches likewise keep evaluation of the condition.

#+begin_sequence :title "A proposed change earns its evidence"
lanes optimizer · untrusted candidate | subject · exact core models | checker · portable proof | caller · explicit precondition
optimizer -> subject : Supply the original and changed programs.
subject -> checker : Rebuild the value, return and named-error relation.
checker --> subject : Check every case and arithmetic contradiction.
subject => caller : Return the changed core only with a checked comparison.
note subject, caller : An unknown, unsupported, vacuous or solver-only answer keeps the original.
#+end_sequence

The proof rules now handle conditionals inside linear integer arithmetic and ground division by a nonzero integer. Known logical guards let the checker ignore a value that cannot be observed after a failure. General nonlinear arithmetic and variable division still need broader evidence; an unproved rewrite stays behind the gate.

Native counterexamples also run through both concrete core programs. When OxiZ leaves an unused input opaque, BL can choose an explicitly labelled completion and recheck the whole counterexample. The chosen value never becomes a claimed native observation.

#+begin_honest :title "Where the gate lives"
This is an implemented gate for core models in the research library. It can start from real compiler ANF, but it does not yet emit optimized BEAM or prove that the compiler preserves those models. The source/core/symbolic correspondence and checker implementation still carry their stated trust boundary.
#+end_honest

The combined core/contract/loop/translation run passed 4,432 assertions, including 244 translation assertions and 784 certificate assertions. Nine translation examples ran on both native backends: checked changes, replayed failures, a vacuous input and a nonlinear identity with no checked certificate. These are finite implementation checks and scoped model arguments.

Read [[file:TRANSLATION.md][the interface and outcome relation]], [[file:src/formal/translation.bl][the independent translation boundary]], [[file:src/formal/optimize.bl][the candidate optimizer]], [[file:evidence/translation/checks.txt][its focused checks]], [[file:evidence/translation/examples.json][the actual examples and proofs]] and [[file:evidence/translation/qualification.json][the qualified source and artifacts]].

** The same conversation for every kind of promise
The public =check=, =compare= and =describe= functions now accept a term question, a program contract, a loop contract, a translation, a protocol state model, a source-derived handler protocol or a bounded trace subject. Each subject is ordinary BL data. The same evidence envelope explains the answer, its certificate, assumptions, input feasibility, source connection and native observations. The original detailed results remain available.

#+begin_rosetta
What you want to know | Subject | What a checked answer concerns
Does this claim follow? | Term question | Its exact assumptions and mathematical profile.
Does this function keep its promise? | Program contract | The pure core model and its declared outcomes.
Does this loop finish with the right result? | Loop contract | Its invariant, ranking argument and exit condition.
Can this change replace that program? | Translation | Both exact core models, including their named failures.
Can every permitted step preserve this promise? | Protocol state model | Initialization, every transition and its declared input assumptions.
Can a short execution break the promise? | Bounded trace subject | Its exact model and depth, with a replayed execution when a witness is found.
#+end_rosetta

Z3 is now called the reference engine in a comparison. That role gives it no compiler authority. A native UNSAT result, a portable checked proof and an actual source-level guarantee remain separate facts in every answer.

The initial common facade passed 199 new assertions; its focused run passed 481 assertions including existing API and translation checks. The protocol extension has its own qualification below. Implementation: [[file:src/formal/api.bl][the shared entry points]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/result.bl][the evidence envelope]] and [[file:evidence/translation/facade-checks.txt][the first common-interface checks]].

** A state promise, across every permitted step
A protocol model now describes named state fields, admitted initial states, message or transition variants, input assumptions, guards and updates. Its checker rebuilds the induction obligations from the closed definition. A proof must cover initialization and every transition. No cached success flag or missing transition proof can replace that coverage.

For an account, we can check that the balance stays nonnegative and that credits minus debits equal the balance. The argument covers every finite prefix of permitted model steps, with no trace-depth limit. Input assumptions say which messages the model admits. Initial feasibility is shown separately by a replayed state or native witness.

#+begin_rosetta
What the model observes | What happens to the state | What the evidence records
A false guard | The transition is disabled. | It cannot occur as an executed trace event.
A failed guard or update | The old state is retained. | The failed attempt stays visible to safety.
A successful update | All fields read the same old state, then commit together. | The next state and invariant are replayed.
An input outside the assumptions | The event is outside the declared environment. | The trace is rejected with that reason.
#+end_rosetta

A failed induction obligation can describe an invariant state that no execution reaches. We call that =obligation-refuted=. A concrete replay is separate evidence: it checks an admitted starting state and an explicit sequence of model events. Failure and stuttering remain visible in that sequence.

The focused run passed 648 assertions, including 133 new protocol assertions and the existing feature, advanced, API and common-interface checks. The state-induction checkpoint has 25 checked theorems. Its three new induction and failure-exclusion theorem audits are empty; the two integer ranking proofs retain their listed standard assumptions. The BL implementation-to-model connection remains reviewed and unproved.

#+begin_honest :title "State safety is one part of an actor promise"
This is an authored state model. It does not yet extract handlers or observe a running BEAM actor. A terminating handler, a service that keeps responding and a process that eventually finishes are different properties. Mailboxes, selective receive, delivery, crashes, external effects and fairness still need their own explicit semantics. No liveness or live actor guarantee follows from these model proofs.
#+end_honest

Six examples ran with both native engines. Their separately authored traces include safe steps, an actual overdraft, guard and update failures, an inadmissible initial state, and a safe zero-step execution alongside an unreachable induction countermodel. The five admissible traces replayed; the empty-domain initial state was rejected. The existing 92 feature and advanced questions kept their prior backend verdicts.

See [[file:PROTOCOLS.md][the protocol interface and execution rules]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/protocol.bl][the independent induction boundary]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/protocol-run.bl][the concrete trace executor]], [[file:evidence/protocol/checks.txt][the focused checks]], [[file:evidence/protocol/examples.json][the exact examples, proofs and traces]], [[file:evidence/protocol/qualification.json][the qualified source and artifacts]] and [[file:evidence/protocol/model-qualification.json][the current model and axiom ledger]].

** A solver answer you can run, step by step
Bounded safety is now a closed subject too. The question carries its exact state model and depth. A proof for depth one cannot stand in for depth two. The decoder reads native choices and inputs, runs the declared model independently, and checks the resulting states against every unrolled assertion and concrete native observation.

Consider a counter starting at zero, increasing by one, with the promise that it stays below two. A depth-one search finds no violation. At depth two, both backends produce a trace through zero, one and two. That answer now includes the actual events and replayed states, rather than only a satisfiable formula.

#+begin_rosetta
What comes back | What BL checks | What we may conclude
An unsafe native model | Initial admission, choices, inputs, every state and the negated safety claim | A valid replay establishes an unsafe trace of this declared model.
An opaque unused scalar | An explicitly labelled completion, followed by the whole replay | The chosen value belongs to replay; it is not a new native observation.
A failed attempt | Old state retained, failure recorded, only stuttering afterwards | Later steps cannot erase that failure.
A repaired final state | Every earlier prefix as well as the final state | An earlier invariant violation still counts.
A safe bounded answer | Its exact bound and any independently checked certificate | It covers that bound; an unbounded argument is a separate subject.
#+end_rosetta

Comparisons also distinguish native status agreement from logical agreement. Two paths may carry the same portable proof while one engine reports unknown. That gives us a shared checked argument and an explicit backend capability gap. A raw backend contradiction remains visible even when portable evidence agrees.

Nine examples ran on both backends: ten unsafe answers became independently replayed traces, four safe answers had checked portable proofs, two safe answers remained solver-only, and two initial domains were vacuous. The combined run passed 473 assertions, including 103 trace assertions; a follow-up adds checks that repairing the final state cannot erase an earlier violation.

The bounded-run checkpoint checks 30 Lean theorems. Five laws formalize finite model runs, stuttering, retained state and persistent failure, including the connection between successful finite runs and reachable states. Their printed axiom audits are empty. The correspondence to the BL unroller and executor remains reviewed and unproved.

See [[file:BOUNDED-TRACES.md][the bounded subject and replay rules]], [[file:src/formal/protocol-trace.bl][the decoder and independent checks]], [[file:evidence/protocol-traces/checks.txt][the combined check log]], [[file:evidence/protocol-traces/prefix-checks.txt][the repaired-prefix follow-up]], [[file:evidence/protocol-traces/examples.json][the actual native answers and replayed executions]], [[file:evidence/protocol-traces/qualification.json][the exact qualified artifacts]] and [[file:evidence/protocol-traces/model-qualification.json][the mathematical model and axiom ledger]].

** A promise about the function you actually wrote
The next connection is now implemented: ordinary record-shaped source functions can carry contracts, and state transitions can be derived from actual handler source. You write a function that reads a state and a message and returns the next state. The interface extracts its real compiler ANF; it does not ask you to rewrite that update as a second model callback.

Records keep one whole-expression outcome. Reading a useful field cannot hide an error in another field. Updates evaluate their arguments in order, and an eager lookup default can still fail when the key is present. These details make the source promise meaningful.

#+begin_sequence :title "The handler earns a source connection"
lanes source · ordinary BL | core · closed records | model · state promises | checker · evidence | run · concrete trace
source -> core : Compile real function ANF and check its record core.
core -> model : Derive every state field and the whole-handler outcome.
model -> checker : Rebuild the source connection and check all induction premises.
checker --> model : Return a source-bound proof object.
model -> run : Replay the model and execute the handler core for each step.
note source, run : Live actor behavior needs its own observation and refinement contract.
#+end_sequence

#+begin_rosetta
Language behavior | What the formal path preserves | Why it matters
An error in an unused field | The whole record raises | Projection cannot manufacture a successful function.
Several updates to the same field | Every argument is evaluated; the last successful value wins | A later update cannot erase an earlier failure.
A handler fails before returning | The old state remains; the attempt stays visible | Even an empty state record cannot hide the error.
A conditional returns the old state | A successful no-change step | It is distinct from a failed attempt.
Two engines inspect a handler | The same source-bound subject and separate native receipts | A common checked proof does not invent native solver support.
#+end_rosetta

The focused source/record run passed 1,292 assertions across six files, including 689 record and 125 source-protocol assertions. That mathematical-model checkpoint checks 42 theorems. Twelve added laws describe finite products, eager defaults, updates and whole-product failures. Thirty-seven theorem audits have no axiom dependencies; three use =propext= and the two integer ranking rules retain their listed standard assumptions.

#+begin_honest :title "A source handler is one part of a running actor"
The current source-protocol path supports flat integer/Boolean state and message records. Function contracts also support nested closed records. The source, core, term model and mathematical products are connected by reviewed implementations; their correspondence is not mechanically proved. A separate sequential actor observer is now implemented below. Mailbox order, independent sending, crashes/restarts, fairness and progress still need broader semantics and refinement work.
#+end_honest

Read [[file:RECORD-HANDLERS.md][the record and source-handler interface]], [[file:src/formal/source-protocol.bl][the rebuilt source connection]], [[file:test/record_test.bl][record semantics checks]], [[file:test/source_protocol_test.bl][source-protocol checks]], [[file:evidence/record-handlers/source-check.txt][the focused check log]] and [[file:evidence/record-handlers/model-check.txt][the actual 42-theorem axiom audit]].

** A counterexample that goes through your handler
The same =protocol-trace= constructor now accepts a source-derived protocol. Its question binds the exact source and depth before either solver sees it. A differently spelled source function gets its own subject, even when it produces the same state model.

An unsafe answer has two execution views. The state model runs the chosen events. Separately, the extracted handler core computes each returned state or named failure. Both traces must agree, every concrete native observation must match, and the entire term context must validate. A missing observation gets a labelled completion and must survive both replays.

#+begin_rosetta
What we inspect | What it must show | The limit remains visible
State-model execution | The initial state, every step and every earlier invariant | A later repair cannot erase an earlier violation.
Handler-core execution | The actual extracted function's state or named failure at each selected step | This is core execution; no live actor was started.
Native observations | Every concrete value agrees with independent replay | Completed values never become claimed native observations.
The exact question | Source identity, semantic profile and maximum depth | A safe depth-one answer does not cover depth two.
#+end_rosetta

The focused run passed 762 assertions, including 159 new source-bound trace checks. Nine examples ran with both engines: six safe results carried checked portable proofs, ten unsafe answers passed both model and handler-core replay, and two initial domains were vacuous. Unsafe initial states need no handler execution; that zero-step case records a handler count of zero.

The separate Lean model now checks 46 theorems. Four new laws describe how typed handler steps and runs refine a state model and inherit its safety. Their axiom audits are empty. The rule explicitly requires universal return and failure correspondence; finite replay tests instances of that correspondence and does not prove it for all inputs. Forty-one theorem audits are empty overall, with the other five retaining the assumptions listed above.

Read [[file:BOUNDED-TRACES.md][the unified trace interface]], [[file:test/source_trace_test.bl][the source-bound replay checks]], [[file:evidence/source-traces/checks.txt][the 762-assertion log]], [[file:evidence/source-traces/examples.json][all eighteen native results and both execution views]], [[file:evidence/source-traces/qualification.json][the exact implementation receipts]] and [[file:evidence/source-traces/model-qualification.json][the 46-theorem model audit]]. The earlier [[file:evidence/record-handlers/examples.json][source-handler induction examples]] and [[file:evidence/record-handlers/implementation-qualification.json][record/source implementation receipt]] remain separate checkpoints.

** Run the handler behind the counterexample
The next bridge runs actual compiled Beam Lisp. It compiles the freshly admitted handler source, retains its exact emitted BEAM, and delivers the selected messages to one owned actor. A separate checker then compares the recorded returns, states and supported named failures against both reference executions.

This makes a solver counterexample directly useful: ask either engine for a bad trace, replay its complete answer, then watch the corresponding handler produce that behavior. Actual execution is an explicit operation. Asking whether a mathematical claim holds does not silently start an actor.

#+begin_sequence :title "From a solver witness to an observed program"
lanes question · exact source and bound | witness · checked concrete trace | actor · exact compiled handler | checker · reconstructed references
question -> witness : Replay the native answer and both source/model views.
witness -> actor : Deliver the admitted messages one at a time.
actor --> checker : Record returns, raw failures and committed states.
checker => question : Report finite conformance separately from safety.
note actor, checker : The actor and temporary modules are retired. Checking the record does not call its handlers.
#+end_sequence

#+begin_rosetta
What you ask | The operation | What the answer says
Run these admitted messages | observe-protocol-trace | Observe exact compiled source through the sequential adapter.
Inspect this saved execution record | check-runtime-observation | Rebuild source, artifacts and reference outcomes; compare ordinary BL data.
Try this native counterexample for real | observe-protocol-counterexample | Replay the solver witness before starting its corresponding actor.
#+end_rosetta

A particularly useful result is /conforming and unsafe/. An unguarded withdrawal really makes the balance negative, exactly as the model predicted. Conformance says the observation matches the model. Safety says whether the promise survived that observation. Neither finite result proves what every future execution will do.

The focused integrated run passed 921 assertions across seven files, including 159 new runtime assertions. The runtime checks cover exact source/artifact binding, altered records, hidden-field and empty-state failures, rollback, deadlines, process death, cleanup and actual counterexamples from both engines. The other 762 assertions repeat the existing source-trace, protocol and common-interface checks.

#+begin_honest :title "What this actor bridge observes"
The adapter delivers messages sequentially and commits only a normally returned, typed state. A modeled integer failure records the old state and deliberately stops the adapter. Stuttering after that is observation padding, not a live actor. The actor inherits the caller's environment; declared intrinsic meanings and the observer remain assumptions, and external effects are outside this observation. SDK/compiler fingerprints and historical runtime differences remain visible. This is no universal compiler, mailbox, crash-recovery or fairness proof.
#+end_honest

Read [[file:RUNTIME-OBSERVATIONS.md][the runtime interface and its exact relation]], [[file:src/formal/runtime-protocol.bl][the producer and separate receipt checker]], [[file:src/formal/runtime-artifact.bl][source/artifact custody]], [[file:src/formal/runtime-outcome.bl][the cause-based failure projection]], [[file:src/formal/runtime-actor.bl][the owned sequential adapter]], [[file:test/runtime_protocol_test.bl][the runtime checks]], [[file:evidence/actor-runtime/integrated-checks.txt][the 921-assertion log]], [[file:evidence/actor-runtime/examples.json][actual observations]], [[file:evidence/actor-runtime/fresh-replay.json][fresh-VM record checks]] and [[file:evidence/actor-runtime/qualification.json][the qualified scope and artifact receipts]].

** A callee's identity changes the mathematics
The actor bridge immediately found a source-interpretation bug. The front end had treated =BeamLisp.Num/div= as integer quotient: its model returned 3 for 7 divided by 2, while the actual compiled handler returned 3.5. The observer reported disagreement.

Source admission now checks reviewed module/function/arity identities. It refuses that floating/decimal operation in the Int/Bool core, keeps integer =quot=, and distinguishes Erlang's Boolean-only =not= from BL's truthiness-based =not=. Other modules cannot borrow a familiar function name's contract. Old source definitions are re-elaborated before their evidence is considered.

#+begin_rosetta
The expression | Its actual meaning | The formal interface
quot 7 2 | Integer quotient: 3 | Supported in the integer core.
BeamLisp.Num/div 7 2 | Floating division: 3.5 | Refused by this source core; needs its own numeric domain.
BL not 7 | Truthiness negation: false | Supported with BL truthiness.
Erlang not 7 | A Boolean-only operation applied to an integer | Refused rather than assigned BL's meaning.
#+end_rosetta

There is still an explicit environment assumption: the compiler leaves variadic =assoc= as a fetched Var. The front end accepts that declared record operation only in its own formal compilation namespace. Freezing or checking that runtime binding is a separate next step. This lets us preserve ordinary updates while making the unresolved boundary visible.

The follow-up passed 4,877 assertions across nine focused files, including 135 callee-identity regressions and the existing core, record, loop, contract, translation and runtime checks. These counts overlap earlier checkpoints; they describe this repair's qualification, not an accumulated proof of soundness.

Read [[file:INTRINSICS.md][the callee catalog and remaining binding boundary]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/intrinsics.bl][the exact remote identities]], [[file:src/formal/elaborate.bl][the source admission rules]], [[file:test/intrinsics_test.bl][the identity and stale-source regressions]], [[file:evidence/intrinsics/intrinsics-probe.json][the original actual-BEAM disagreement]], [[file:evidence/intrinsics/checks.txt][the current focused checks]], [[file:evidence/intrinsics/fresh-replay.json][prior observations checked through the repaired front end]] and [[file:evidence/intrinsics/qualification.json][the repair's exact qualified scope]].

** A formal program carries its meanings
Even the right function name can resolve to a different implementation. The next probe replaced only =assoc= in an owned child environment. The formal state update stayed the same, but the ordinary compiled handler returned a replacement state. The observer caught that disagreement too.

An explicit binding context now describes a formal function as BL data: its source, typed parameters, core identity and fixed record-call bindings. The same declaration can back a function contract or a source protocol. The linker preserves eager argument order and whole-record failures, then reconstructs the resulting core. The original and linked canonical cores must agree.

For a protocol, =:bindings :closed-records= opts into that meaning. The ordinary binding mode remains available. The context belongs to the declared program: saying it is equivalent to an ambient program still requires the ambient bindings to have the declared meanings.

#+begin_code_graph :id formal-binding-context :title "One declaration, from meaning to observation" :start declaration
node declaration | concept | The function and its meaning | implemented
summary Source, ordered parameter types and record bindings travel as ordinary BL data.
field Identity | Reconstructed from the complete declaration; no function, PID or Env handle is stored.
source research/formal-logic/src/formal/context.bl | formal.context/define | Creates the explicit binding context.
node linker | code | Resolve the declared record calls | implemented

The owned child-Env check now has a useful comparison: the ordinary handler calls the replacement and disagrees; the explicitly linked handler returns the modeled state without calling that replacement. All actors and temporary artifacts are retired. Two engines also check the context-backed function contract and supply unsafe contextual traces that run through actual BEAM.

The first context run passed 5,494 assertions across fourteen files. The canonical-identity follow-up below passed 7,099 assertions across 22 focused files, including 139 context checks. The separate mathematical model still has 46 theorems. This implementation does not discharge its universal source/runtime simulation premises or grant compiler authority.

#+begin_honest :title "How much of the world is closed?"
The function's ANF no longer fetches the selected record Vars. The SDK functions it calls still have implementations and dependencies, and source compilation still has a macro environment. The context makes that next boundary explicit; it does not establish a closed transitive runtime, absence of external effects or equivalence under arbitrary ambient overrides.
#+end_honest

** Name the actual function and its world
A promise can now point to a real named Source function: /withdraw in accounts, revision v1/. The SDK selects its exact definitions. A private helper stays private, a captured fee keeps its value, and a same-spelled ambient Var cannot replace either one.

This moves the formal interface closer to ordinary programs. You supply the Source units and typed input roles; the existing contract API can ask Z3 and OxiZ the same question. Imported revisions and Source-defined hygienic macros use the SDK's actual binding and expansion machinery.

#+begin_rosetta
The declaration | What it selects | What the evidence covers
A source string with declared intrinsics | A function under an explicit get/assoc binding policy | The linked core and its stated equivalence precondition.
A selected Source context | A named public function, exact revisions, lexical helpers and captured values | Initializing that unit, then one call through its specialized core.
A runtime observation | An owned source-handler artifact and finite events | The recorded states and outcomes; universal refinement remains open.
#+end_rosetta

#+begin_note :title "Initialization belongs to the promise"
Suppose an unused top-level value divides by zero. The selected unit cannot finish initializing, even when withdraw never reads that value. The model preserves that failure. A future contract for repeated calls on an already initialized instance will need a separate scope.
#+end_note

The coherent follow-up passed 7,266 assertions across 23 files, including 118 selected-Source checks, 62 source-expression checks and 108 loop checks. The selected cases cover explicit imports, a hygienic macro, nested records, ambient shadowing and both engines. Refused forms include effects on the selected path, unresolved calls, recursive control and escaping functions. The new specializer has no mechanical preservation theorem, and the native leaf implementations retain their runtime assumptions.

This work also found and repaired the old name-only callee policy in the second, semantic-value source front end. Its retained probe modeled division as 3 while the actual numeric function returned 3.5, and modeled strict Erlang negation on 7 as a successful false while the actual call raised. Both source front ends now share the exact callee catalog. Qualified foreign inputs also cannot borrow a local input's meaning.

Four stored selected contexts also reconstruct in a fresh VM. Eight conditional BL certificates recheck and two native counterexamples replay against the reconstructed core. The checking window opens no native backend session; a separate real session calibrates that trace first. This establishes portable reconstruction and evidence checking for these examples, with no selected runtime initializer or target invocation.

** Run the function behind the promise
The promise can now lead to a real call. Beam Lisp keeps the original Source unit, lets its normal SDK owner open the actual instance, invokes the selected export and records the outcome. Then it retires that instance and its code.

The formal core gives us something precise to compare with that record. A good call can match and keep its promise. A buggy call can also match the model perfectly /and violate its promise/. Those are different answers, and the interface reports both.

#+begin_rosetta
The question | The evidence now records | What stays open
Which function was invoked? | The original artifact, selected export and normal Source-instance owner. | An execution attestation independent of the observer.

#+end_rosetta

The coherent follow-up passed 7,396 assertions across 24 files, including 130 for this runtime path. Eight retained observations conform to the core; three violate their promises. One factory fails during initialization, so only seven target calls occur. The imported macro still reaches its selected private helper, and the call-error record retains its real zero-divisor even with no application frame in the stack.

The eight records also recheck in a fresh VM. A separate calibration observes one initializer, one export and one application-module load. The subsequent checking window records /zero/ of each. This is portable recorded-data checking with an explicit observer assumption; universal refinement remains unproved.

** Rename an input without changing the function
Calling an input /amount/ should give you the same function as calling it /input/. The implementation had a real counterexample: choosing the role =local_3= made the lowered model return 20 for a function that actually returned 17.

The proof checker had checked the arithmetic of that model. The mistake happened earlier, while source bindings became Core variables. That is why the connection to source needs its own evidence.

#+begin_rosetta
The identity | Example | What it means
Source spelling | a | The parameter written by the programmer.
Compiler alias | a_2 or local_3 | A lexical identity invented during compilation.
Semantic role | "local_3" | The caller's name for the mathematical input; it may share another identity's spelling.
Core binder | A fresh internal identity | A local in the formal program, distinct from all referenced input roles.
#+end_rosetta

** Keep a helper's own environment
A helper remembers where it was defined. If it captured a fee of 40, a caller's local fee of 100 must not change that helper. With input 2, the intended result is 42.

We now check the earlier step that specializes the selected Source unit into ANF. An adversarial example replaces the captured reference with the caller's local one. The resulting model returns 102. It is a perfectly consistent ANF/Core pair, so the later check accepts that pair. The new unit/ANF check rejects it against the original program.

#+begin_rosetta
The environment | Which expression uses it? | In this example
Caller's environment | The eager argument expressions. | A caller can have its own local fee of 100.
Definition environment | The helper's body and captured values. | The helper retains its fee of 40.
Original selected Source | The actual compiled function, called with input 2. | Returns 42 in the retained observation.
Altered specialization | A proposed model that wrongly borrows the caller's fee. | Returns 102; rejected before it can become this Source function's formal model.
#+end_rosetta

** Check the translation before proving the promise
A proof needs the right model. We now have a separate check for the step that turns compiler ANF into the formal Core. It reads both trees and checks how their pieces correspond. It never asks the elaborator to generate the answer again.

This gives two distinct pieces of evidence. One says that the proposed Core follows the admitted translation rules. The other says that a promise follows from that Core and its assumptions. Neither can stand in for the other.

#+begin_rosetta
The check | What it establishes | What still needs a connection
ANF/Core relation | Bindings, operands, branches and reviewed calls match the proposed Core. | Source expansion, selected-unit specialization and the checker's soundness.
Conditional term proof | The stated Core promise follows under the declared assumptions. | The proof checker's soundness and the implementation of Core semantics.
Original Source observation | This particular compiled call produced the recorded outcome. | Universal behavior, observer trust and runtime refinement.
Lean preservation theorem | A typed mathematical ANF translation preserves every returned or named-error outcome. | The SDK's named ANF and the BL validator are not yet encoded in that proof.
#+end_rosetta

** Name the model and its connections
The same Core program can be written directly as data or extracted from BL Source. Those two starting points need different labels. A checked promise about authored Core supplies a mathematical model claim. A Source-connected promise also carries its reviewed extraction or linking step.

Both routes then need a faithful interpretation of the model into term questions. That implementation step is now a separate record. It names the exact input model and the generated questions, with their identities, scopes and profiles. Its status says /reviewed, not mechanically proved/. It cannot replace a certificate.

#+begin_rosetta
What you supply | Source connection | Model interpretation
A term question | No BL Source claim to establish. | Already terms; no earlier model interpretation applies.
Core data | Authored Core, with no BL Source claim. | The reviewed Core-to-whole-outcome question mapping.
An actual Source function or context | Reviewed extraction or linking, plus the scoped translation checks. | The same separate Core-to-question mapping.
A state model, loop or trace | Authored or Source-extracted, as its profile declares. | Reviewed induction, ranking or bounded-safety questions.
#+end_rosetta

source research/formal-logic/evidence/connections/qualification.json | Qualified scope | Keeps changed assurance labels separate from unchanged propositions.
edge model -> questions | records the interpretation step
edge source -> model | adds a Source connection when supplied
edge questions -> proof | gives the certificate its exact proposition
edge questions -> history | permits reproducible reconstruction
edge proof -> boundary | retains implementation premises
edge history -> boundary | reproducibility is its own evidence
#+end_code_graph

The new field lives at =evidence / connection / model-interpretation= for every model subject. A direct term question reports that this earlier step does not apply. A failed preparation reports that it has not been established. A reviewed connection remains reviewed when its promise is unknown, refuted or vacuous; it does not fill in missing reasoning.

The coherent run passed 8,396 assertions across 28 files, including 189 connection checks. All 187 backend questions keep their previous verdicts. Four earlier certificates, two earlier counterexamples and the original Source observation still check against their unchanged subjects. Four saved records reconstruct in a fresh VM with zero native sessions in the positively calibrated reconstruction window. This adds no theorem that the interpretation implementation is sound.

** A proof checker with a proof
The interesting question is no longer just /did a solver say yes?/ We can now ask /what small set of rules accepted this proof, and why are those rules sound?/

There is an executable Boolean and integer checker in Lean. Its soundness theorem says that /every proof it accepts rules out every model of its normalized problem/. Eleven actual BL certificate exports have gone through that exact checker. This is a universal result about the checked rules, applied to real BL data.

#+begin_rosetta
Step | What happens | What it establishes
BL export | Reconstruct the original question and certificate as a closed packet. | A reviewed connection to the normalized data; export itself runs no Lean process.
Data bridge | Recompute the packet identity and independently decode the generated Lean constructors. | The checked source contains that packet's normalized problem and proof.
Lean check | Compute acceptance with =by decide= and apply the general soundness theorem. | The normalized problem has no integer/Boolean model.
Next bridge | Prove that term normalization preserves the original question. | Still needed to carry that theorem all the way back to original BL terms.
#+end_rosetta

#+end_code_graph

The examples include an integer bound over two thousand bits, negative division and modulus, piecewise choices, and a variable name containing quotes and code-like text. None of those names become Lean code. Rehashing an altered proof cannot make Lean accept it; rehashing a packet cannot make the BL reconstruction connect it to a different original question.

This does not prove that all assumptions are feasible. An inconsistent set of assumptions still permits a conditional claim. It also does not add certificates for every solver theory. The small kernel gives us a precise, working foundation to extend one semantic bridge at a time.

That normalized-only checkpoint passed 8,504 assertions across 29 files, including 108 new export and subject-binding checks. All 187 shared backend questions retain their per-case verdicts. The separate Lean integration check accepts the eleven actual packets and rejects the five altered refutations. The next chapter adds checked connections for the original typed questions.

** Carry the proof back to the question
When =x + 1 > x= becomes a constant fact during normalization, =x= disappears from the final row table. The original question still matters. Change only the right-hand =x= to another input, and the old proof must stop working.

BL now keeps those original assertion and claim trees beside the normalized proof. For each accepted packet, Lean checks that /any counterexample to the original question would also satisfy the normalized problem/. The refutation rules that out. The resulting theorem covers every integer and Boolean input of that particular typed question.

Seventeen real exports now have those original-term proofs. Five altered original questions are rejected even though their old normalized refutations still check. The changes include dropping assumptions, replacing the claim with false, changing a canceled input, selecting the wrong branch, and making the claim divide by zero.

#+begin_rosetta
Layer | What it says | What remains separate
Original typed terms | The contained mathematical claim follows from its original assertions, for all inputs. | The reviewed interpretation of BL term data as this typed model.
Per-question connection | An original countermodel would satisfy this exact normalized problem. | A general theorem that every BL normalization rule is implemented correctly.
Normalized refutation | No model satisfies that normalized problem. | Source extraction, compiler preservation and BEAM behavior.
Saved full query | Fresh BL reconstruction connects the packet to its exact query and certificate. | A self-consistent digest alone supplies no execution or Source provenance.
#+end_rosetta

#+end_code_graph

Undefined arithmetic is part of the promise too. A checked claim must return /true/ in this mathematical term model; returning no value cannot pass. A dead division-by-zero branch can remain irrelevant because the model defines selected branches and Boolean dominance explicitly. This is separate from BL program effects and exceptions.

The proof generator has finite search and transport limits. The theorem's integers are unbounded, and its quantified inputs are universal. Failure to build a proof remains /not accepted/; it is not a counterexample. The new theorem also retains its assumptions: inconsistent assumptions can still support a conditional promise without establishing that they are feasible.

This checkpoint passed 8,558 BL assertions across 30 files, including 54 new original-tree binding checks. All 187 shared backend questions retain their verdicts. Seventeen saved term packets reconstruct in a fresh VM with zero native sessions in the positively calibrated reconstruction window. Those data checks remain separate from the seventeen recorded Lean proofs.

** One proof plan for a whole promise
A countdown promise needs eight separate reasons: the initial state fits, its invariant survives a step, the rank decreases, and returning preserves the result. Those questions now travel together as a closed BL value. A checker rebuilds the plan from the subject and checks every required entry.

The same interface covers direct questions, contracts, Core changes, loops, state protocols, Source handlers and bounded traces. Supply certificates by label or let BL search for them. Each entry keeps its exact question and its own status. A missing premise remains visible.

#+begin_stats
7 | subject kinds | one obligation-plan interface
13 | exported examples | eleven complete bundles and two incomplete
22 | required term proofs | twenty-one entailments and one unreachable path
1 | auxiliary inconsistency proof | separate from a positive witness
#+end_stats

#+begin_rosetta
Evidence | What it closes | What it does not fill in
Property theorem | The exact conditional term promise. | Missing evidence for another required row.
Unreachable-path theorem | The path's assertions cannot all hold. | A claim that the path can actually run.
Initial inconsistency theorem | No state satisfies the contained initial term context. | A positive initial witness.
SAT witness request | An existence question that needs a witness. | An empty or missing proof is never a successful witness.
#+end_rosetta

A proof has foundations as well as program preconditions. Common bundles name a standard-Lean or choice-free policy. The report lists the actual dependencies of every checked theorem. Choosing a policy constrains acceptable proofs; the program's runtime effects remain a separate part of its meaning.

One important result is deliberately still incomplete: a direct SAT question needs a witness. A conditional model promise can have complete required proofs while its initial-state witness remains unavailable. The table keeps those two jobs distinct. The vacuous contract has a checked initial inconsistency proof; the dead transition has an unreachable-path proof. They keep different labels.

The integrated run passed 8,860 assertions across 31 files, including 302 coverage and policy checks. All 187 backend questions retain their verdicts. Thirteen archived bundles reconstruct in another VM; both counted entries are positively calibrated first, then remain zero during full-plan checking and supplied-certificate packaging. Source preparation stays allowed in that window.

** Show that a state can exist
A promise can be true whenever its assumptions hold even if those assumptions never hold. To show that a program's starting conditions are possible, we need another kind of evidence: actual values that satisfy them.

Take the condition x × y = 42. Choosing 6 for x and 7 for y makes it true. Beam Lisp can now package those values with the original typed terms, replay them independently, and ask the pinned Lean checker to prove that this exact set of conditions has a satisfying assignment. The packet stays ordinary language data.

#+begin_rosetta
The question | The evidence | What it establishes
Does the promise follow? | Conditional entailment proof | Every admitted input satisfies the stated promise.
Can these conditions hold? | Positive SAT witness | Explicit values satisfy every original condition.
Can this path occur? | Refutation of its path conditions | No input satisfies these path conditions.
Did this Source code run that way? | Scoped runtime observation | The recorded owned execution agrees within its stated scope.
#+end_rosetta

Thirty-two original packets pass the external existence checker: twenty-two authored assignments and ten native models, five from each backend. The same checker rejects six changed false or undefined assignments. The general witness model and every applied theorem print their actual logical dependencies; none admits =Classical.choice=. The integrated run passed 9,213 assertions across 33 BL files, and all 187 earlier backend questions retain their verdicts.

#+begin_note :title "Concrete values open a wider fragment"
A universal nonlinear theorem may be beyond the current entailment producer. Checking that 6 × 7 equals 42 is a much smaller job. Concrete witness checking also handles a supplied nonzero divisor and twenty Boolean inputs without enumerating every possible assignment. This broadens positive evidence; it does not turn those examples into universal proofs.
#+end_note

Undefined arithmetic keeps the existing partial meaning. Division by zero cannot itself satisfy an assertion. A selected safe branch, known false conjunction operand or known true disjunction operand can make a dead undefined expression irrelevant. Observation-only terms can remain unavailable without spoiling a valid assignment. A shared relation graph can still go straight to either native engine; the expanded evidence packet has its own size limits.

** Give the proof a real starting point
A countdown proof can tell us that initialization succeeds for every input meeting its precondition. The binding n = 3 supplies one such input. The bundle carries the proof and the binding with the exact questions they answer. Lean checks the binding against the initialization proof's original assumptions, then applies that proof at n = 3.

#+begin_rosetta
The bundle has | What the checker can establish | Example
An entailment and a matching witness | The assumptions and claim hold together for explicit values. | Countdown initialization at n = 3
A witness and an open proof obligation | A starting input exists. The conditional promise needs more evidence. | An unrestricted input whose output is required to be positive
An inconsistency proof | The initial assumptions exclude every input. | A contract that requires false
A required SAT witness | The original SAT question has a model. | The same term question sent to Z3 and OxiZ
#+end_rosetta

The qualification checked 39 term leaves and applied 11 entailments to witnesses across 17 exports. Fifteen bundles have complete required coverage. The integration run passed 9,267 assertions across 34 files, with all 187 earlier backend verdicts preserved.

** Check what the Core terms mean
Consider a record lookup with a default expression that raises an error. The record already has the requested field. Core still evaluates the default as a call argument, so the lookup raises. Its symbolic outcome must say the same thing.

The checker now takes the original Core tree and the actual terms used by the contract machinery. Lean proves that they agree for every typed input to that tree. The proof checks whether it returns, the value on return and the name of a raised error.

#+begin_rosetta
What Core does | What the terms must establish
Returns a value | The success term is true, the payload has that value and the error code is zero.
Raises a named error | The success term is false and the error code names the same error.
Evaluates several operands | The first raised operand determines the error.
Chooses a branch | Only the selected branch contributes an outcome.
#+end_rosetta

#+begin_code_graph :id formal-core-term-correspondence :title "Check the Core tree and the terms used by its contract" :start tree
node tree | code | Retain the original Core tree | implemented
summary The export keeps the declared inputs, original tree, error catalogue and exact symbolic outcome. A prepared contract also binds its question identity.
source research/formal-logic/src/formal/core-terms.bl | Core and contract export | Fresh reconstruction checks the original subject and the whole packet.
node order | code | Preserve evaluation order in a record | observed
summary The contained Core model evaluates operands in their written order. It then assembles the record in named field slots.
field Example | A present field can still have a default that raises. An earlier update that raises stops evaluation even if a later update would overwrite its field.
source research/formal-logic/evidence/core-terms/execution/eager-default/Check.lean | Checked eager-default example | The proof covers every typed input record.
node arithmetic | code | Check the signed arithmetic formulas | observed
summary The solver terms use Euclidean division. Core uses a toward-zero quotient, a dividend-sign remainder and a divisor-sign modulus. The checked arithmetic lemmas connect those formulas.
field Division by zero | The proof checks the named zero-divisor error. Successful payloads are checked on the returning branch.
source research/formal-logic/metatheory/CoreTerms.lean | Checked outcome and arithmetic laws | The audits list the logical dependencies used by each law.
node theorem | code | Prove agreement for every typed input | observed
summary Lean compares evaluation of the original typed tree with the exact emitted success, payload and error terms. The error catalogue has a separate finite round-trip check.
field Scope | The named-data codec and the connection from Source to Core remain reviewed. The theorem applies to the contained typed tree and symbolic outcome.
source research/formal-logic/evidence/core-terms/execution/source-record-contract/Check.lean | Applied source-record contract proof | The selected Core tree and lowered terms come from actual contract preparation.
node rejected | code | Reject a changed meaning | observed
summary Six well-formed substitutions fail the checker. They include reversed record evaluation order, a changed error name and a Euclidean quotient in place of the Core quotient.
source research/formal-logic/evidence/core-terms/execution/rejected.json | Semantic substitution checks | Each attempted packet has its own recomputed identity.
node fresh | code | Reconnect the packet after transfer | observed
summary A fresh Beam Lisp VM reconstructs the original packet. It checks the exact exported data even when a changed placeholder would have no effect on a raised outcome.
source research/formal-logic/evidence/core-terms/fresh/core-terms-fresh.json | Rechecked original subjects | The checking window records zero calls to the counted proof-search and native session entries.
edge tree -> order | binds the actual operand order
edge tree -> arithmetic | carries the emitted signed arithmetic terms
edge order -> theorem | compares both outcomes
edge arithmetic -> theorem | applies the checked formulas
edge theorem -> rejected | exercises incorrect alternatives
edge tree -> fresh | binds the full original data again
#+end_code_graph

The checker accepted 34 original exports, including four contracts. The six rejected substitutions change a successful value, return status, named error, record operand order, signed quotient or returned record field. The failure placeholder has a separate check because a raised outcome has no returned value. The integration run passed 9,426 assertions across 35 files. All 187 earlier backend verdicts matched.

** Prove the promise on the Core program
Suppose a function promises to return a larger integer whenever =x= is nonnegative and a flag is set. A solver can establish the inequality over symbolic terms. To prove the promise about the program, we also need to show that those terms describe what Core actually evaluates.

The new checker connects the two proofs. It checks the entire question, including the precondition, the return policy and the allowed error names. Then it applies the result to the original Core tree for every admitted input. A separate witness, with =x= set to 4 and the flag true, proves there is at least one such input.

#+begin_rosetta
The contract asks | The checked theorem gives
Does the precondition hold? | Every input with a true precondition is covered. A supplied witness proves that at least one input qualifies.
Must this return? | The checked policy rules out a raised outcome. Core returns a value that satisfies the postcondition.
May this raise? | A return still satisfies the postcondition. A raised error must be one of the permitted names.
Can either solver help? | Z3 and OxiZ can supply candidate inputs through the same term-based feasibility question.
#+end_rosetta

#+begin_code_graph :id formal-core-contract-composition :title "Connect the checked question to the program" :start subject
node subject | code | Keep the exact Core program and its policy | implemented
summary The BL packet retains the original Core tree, the entire typed question, the policy terms and an optional witness.
source research/formal-logic/src/formal/core-contracts.bl | Contract export and reconstruction | The same data can be prepared again from the original definition and certificate.
node layout | code | Match proof inputs by name and sort | observed
summary The proof can omit unused inputs. The example puts an unused integer and Boolean before the ones it needs. Both proof slots move from index zero to index one in the Core layout.
field Equality checked by Lean | The remapped question must match the exact assumptions and the whole outcome policy.
source research/formal-logic/evidence/core-contracts/execution/unused-slots/CoreContractCheck.lean | The applied slot and contract proof | The generated file contains the equality, the conditional theorem and the admitted outcome theorem.
node outcome | code | Prove the promise about the Core outcome | observed
summary When the precondition holds, a return satisfies the postcondition and a raised error belongs to the permitted names. A must-return policy rules out the raised case.
field Scope | The theorem evaluates the contained Core program. The named-data codec and Source-to-Core connection remain reviewed.
source research/formal-logic/metatheory/CoreContract.lean | Checked composition rule | The theorem imports the precise Core outcome semantics.
node admission | code | Show there is an admitted input | observed
summary An optional witness supplies the inputs used by the precondition. The checker verifies it and applies the universal theorem to that input.
source research/formal-logic/evidence/core-contracts/execution/accepted.json | Eight checked contracts and six admission proofs | The report records the axiom policy and both premise objects.
node changed | code | Reject a changed promise | observed
summary Lean rejected a changed precondition, a changed postcondition, a changed return policy, a dropped permitted error and a witness that failed the precondition.
source research/formal-logic/evidence/core-contracts/execution/rejected.json | Rejected substitutions | The premise proofs still checked for these five packets. The composed proof failed.
edge subject -> layout | align both typed input layouts
edge layout -> outcome | require the entire question to agree
edge outcome -> admission | apply the theorem to the admitted input
edge outcome -> changed | check that a changed promise fails
#+end_code_graph

#+begin_note :title "What totality means here"
For a finite Core program with a must-return policy, the theorem gives a successful outcome for every admitted input. A contract may also give a precise account of deliberate failure. Ordinary loops need a checked decrease argument, and actors need their own account of messages, scheduling and effects. Source and BEAM preservation remain open.
#+end_note

Seven examples pass the choice-free axiom policy. Signed quotient correspondence in the division example uses Lean's standard policy and records its dependence on =Classical.choice=.

A fresh VM exposed an ordering bug in the two-error contract. The declaration used a set, and the proof question inherited its iteration order. Preparation now sorts the error codes before building the question, so the saved definition can reproduce the certificate's statement.

The [[#cell-story-core-contract-composition][executable example]] shows which inputs the admission witness needs and how BL rejects a changed return policy.

** Prove that a rewrite preserves the outcome
The optimizer turns multiplication by zero into a short sequence. It evaluates the other operand first, then returns zero. In this example the operand returns an integer when a flag is true and raises a named error otherwise. The two versions must agree on both inputs.

Lean checks the original tree, the rewritten tree and the original term question. It proves that the two trees give the same outcome whenever the rewrite's precondition holds. The inputs line up by name and type even when the proof only needs some of them.

#+begin_rosetta
Try it with | What both Core programs do
Flag true and input 7 | Return zero.
Flag false and input 7 | Raise the named error =:phase/left=.
A nested record result | Return equal values in every field, including nested fields.
An impossible precondition | The conditional theorem holds vacuously. The optimizer refuses this false-precondition example.
#+end_rosetta

The [[#cell-story-core-translation-composition][executable example]] runs both trees on a returning input and a failing one. Nine translations passed. They include a signed quotient, nested and empty records, an eager default and a guarded rewrite whose proof omits unused inputs. The signed quotient records a dependence on =Classical.choice=. The other eight passed the choice-free policy.

** Prove that the loop reaches its exit
A countdown starts with two values. The counter starts at the input, and the accumulator starts at zero. Each recurrence reduces the counter and increases the accumulator. The invariant keeps the counter nonnegative and their sum equal to the input. When the loop exits, the accumulator is the answer.

Lean now applies that argument to the exact finite initializer and controller. A finite return trace exists for every admitted input, and the result meets the postcondition at its exit state. The theorem uses no fixed iteration bound.

#+begin_rosetta
What the checker needs | What it proves about this loop
Initialization | The actual initial values satisfy the invariant.
A recurrence | All arguments return normally, and the next state is assigned simultaneously. The invariant holds again and the rank decreases.
An exit | The actual returned value satisfies the postcondition for that exit state.
Two ranks | The outer rank can decrease while the inner rank resets upward.
#+end_rosetta

#+begin_code_graph :id formal-core-loop-composition :title "Follow the proof from initialization to exit" :start init
node init | code | Evaluate the actual initializer | observed
summary The packet retains the original loop control. The independent checker reconstructs the finite initializer and controller and checks their symbolic outcomes.
source research/formal-logic/src/formal/core-loops.bl | Whole-controller term question | Uses the original precondition, invariant, rank and postcondition.
node step | code | Apply the exact recurrence arguments | observed
summary The controller yields a tagged exit or recurrence. Lean checks that substitution into the invariant and rank uses the actual next values.
field State update | Each recurrence argument reads the same current state. State slots are updated together.
source research/formal-logic/metatheory/CoreLoop.lean | State substitution and exact composition | The term entailment is matched to the original initializer and step.
node rank | code | Run on states satisfying the invariant | observed
summary The evaluator only follows recurrence edges whose next state satisfies the invariant. The checked decrease gives a well-founded order for these edges.
source research/formal-logic/metatheory/LoopTotal.lean | Unbounded ranked evaluator and return traces | A trace witnesses successful steps and a normal exit.
node exit | code | Prove the exit postcondition | observed
summary The countdown theorem covers every nonnegative input. It establishes an exit state and a returned value meeting the original postcondition.
field Scope | The theorem covers the contained Core control. Source extraction and BEAM preservation remain open.
source research/formal-logic/evidence/core-loops/execution/countdown/CoreLoopCheck.lean | The applied countdown theorem | Uses the exact initializer, controller, and typed question.
node changed | code | Refuse an altered rank or postcondition | observed
summary The composed proof fails when either of those predicates is changed and the original entailment is supplied again.
source research/formal-logic/evidence/core-loops/execution/rejected.json | Rejected substitutions | Both Core proofs and the original term proof still check.
edge init -> step | start in the invariant
edge step -> rank | check a strictly decreasing edge
edge rank -> exit | establish a finite return trace
edge exit -> changed | test the exact proof connection
#+end_code_graph

The [[#cell-story-core-loop-composition][executable example]] counts down from four. The applied proof covers all admitted inputs. Other examples include a two-rank reset, Boolean state, a simultaneous swap, and a failing expression excluded by the invariant. The reports record the standard Lean axioms used by the integer ranking proof.

The whole-controller proof can refuse a question containing undefined arithmetic in an unreachable return value. The separate path checker can still prove that the branch is unreachable.

** Keep the actor invariant across every admitted event
A bank starts with a zero balance. A deposit or withdrawal must keep the balance nonnegative and equal to credits minus debits. The handler for an oversized withdrawal returns the old state. A negative amount falls outside the declared message assumptions.

Lean checks what each exact Core handler does on every admitted message. It then proves that the invariant holds at every finite trace prefix. The proof includes idle events and uses no fixed trace length.

#+begin_rosetta
An event in the model | What the trace records
A deposit of ten | The handler returns a new state with ten more in both balance and credits.
A withdrawal beyond the balance | The handler returns the old state. The invariant still holds.
A message outside its assumptions | Admission excludes that event from the modeled trace.
A handler raises | The state stays unchanged and the trace records the named failure. Only idle events can follow it.
#+end_rosetta

The proof evaluates the contained Core handlers and uses the declared event relation. Source extraction and correspondence with a live BEAM actor remain open. The initial predicate can be checked separately for feasibility.
#+end_note

** Follow one recorded actor run into Core
The bank example also runs in an owned BEAM actor. It records the starting balance, the messages actually delivered and the state each handler returns. The independent checker takes that recording and proves that each prefix is a trace of the exact Core handlers. The bank's all-traces theorem then applies to each of those prefixes.

#+begin_rosetta
What happened in the run | What the independent checker can establish
The bank reaches a balance of seven | The starting state and all four events agree with the exact Core trace, including the idle event.
The divisor is zero | The named failure retains the previous state. An idle event can follow it and keeps that failure recorded.
A handler writes a negative value and later restores it | The first bad prefix violates the invariant. The later repair leaves that earlier violation in the trace.
#+end_rosetta

#+begin_note :title "What the recording can tell us"
The proof is about the recorded values in the exact Core model. Beam Lisp checks their connection to the supplied receipt, and the observer is trusted to report the execution faithfully. Proving that the compiler and scheduler always produce these traces is a separate obligation.

** Follow a counterexample from either solver
A solver finds a withdrawal that sends the balance below zero. Beam Lisp can now keep the original question, the solver's values and the recorded handler run together as ordinary language data. The independent checker proves that those values make the original claim false and that the same values appear in an unsafe Core trace.

#+begin_rosetta
The connection | What gets checked
The original question | Every assumption holds and the exact original claim evaluates to false.
The values passed to the handler | The selected handler, its typed message and every state agree with the query's bindings.
A value the engine leaves opaque | Replay supplies an explicit completion and retains its origin. The concrete value still goes through the checks.
A different unsafe run | Both premise proofs can succeed. The final equality check refuses a run with different values.
#+end_rosetta

#+begin_note :title "How far this argument reaches"
The result establishes one original query counterexample and its recorded Core execution. Native discovery is recorded as claimed engine provenance, and execution reporting retains the observer assumption. The general correspondence between the unroller and every admitted trace remains open.

** Ask through the language library
The formal interface now reaches the SDK. A program can ask =system/preserves?= about a declared state model, select Z3 or OxiZ, and inspect the same portable question before either engine runs. Portable certificate checking is available through the same entry point.

This makes the experiment easier to use in an ordinary Beam Lisp program. The term, question, proof and state-model modules have one canonical implementation in the SDK. Research imports resolve those namespaces from the selected SDK.

#+begin_rosetta
What you want to do | The language interface | What travels with it
Inspect the promise | system.formal/preservation-question | The exact claim, assumptions, observations and semantic profile.
Check a model | system/prove-box | Initial-state evidence and complete establishment and preservation obligations.
Compare engines | system/preserves? with a backend option | The same question, each native verdict and its evidence basis.
Handle solver access | oracle/with-query-handler | The complete question and a scoped native fallback.
Read a counterexample | The common evidence envelope | Values replayed against the original assumptions and goal.
#+end_rosetta

source research/formal-logic/SDK-INTERFACE.md | SDK interface guide | Public examples and the remaining migration boundary.

edge question -> effect | carry the whole question
edge effect -> engines | delegate within the owned context
