Read [[file:WORD-EXHAUSTION-PROTOTYPE.md][the finite checker and its trust boundary]], [[file:evidence/word-exhaustion-prototype/qualification.json][its actual qualification]], [[file:evidence/word-exhaustion-prototype/native.json][both native claims and independent checking records]], [[file:TEXT-RESOURCE-PROTOTYPE.md][the explicit text interfaces]], [[file:evidence/text-resource-prototype/qualification.json][their actual qualification]], [[file:evidence/text-resource-prototype/native.json][the recorded text capability gap]], [[file:EVIDENCE-BUNDLE-PROTOTYPE.md][the evidence composition contract]], [[file:evidence/evidence-bundle-prototype/qualification.json][the complete bundle gate]], [[file:evidence/evidence-bundle-prototype/reader.json][the fresh solver-denied reader]] and [[file:SCALAR-TEXT-INTEGRATION-PLAN.md][the next runtime boundary design]].

** Keep every assumption with the question
A solver session remembers facts. Your next question may mention only some of them. Beam Lisp can now freeze the complete question before execution: the caller's terms, every retained assumption, the checking policy and the requested backend settings.

Suppose the session remembers that =x= is =7= and the caller asks for =x ≤ 9=. The point where =x= is =8= fits the caller's line but breaks the remembered fact. The [[#cell-story-retained-request][executed example]] rejects it against the complete question. The same frozen question can go through the existing owner, a fresh Z3 or OxiZ owner, a supplied point handler, or a saved-record reader.

#+begin_rosetta :title "What travels, and what stays local"
Piece | What it contains | What you can rely on
Portable request | Complete question, policy and adapter settings | Every retained assumption is part of the subject.
Recorded composition | Caller graph, retained graph, declared generation and revision | The complete bytes can be rebuilt and checked.
Local execution callback | Actual owner and captured guard | Same-process, single-use execution during its body.
Saved native answer | A status claim and any returned coordinates | Coordinates get fresh replay; native history remains recorded data.
#+end_rosetta

#+begin_graph :title "Keep meaning portable, execution local"
node caller | Caller question | Original terms | 0,0 | you
node retained | Retained facts | Explicit assumptions | 0,1 | station
node complete | Complete question | Portable graph data | 1,0 | door
node choice | Execution choice | Owner or handler | 2,0 | builder
node replay | Fresh replay | Check the full point | 3,0 | you
edge caller -> complete | freeze | solid
edge retained -> complete | include | solid
edge complete -> choice | ask | solid
edge choice -> replay | inspect | solid
#+end_graph

#+begin_sequence :title "A remembered fact never becomes a hidden premise"
lanes caller · your program | owner · live context | handler · request effect | reader · fresh checking
caller -> owner : Read actual facts and adapter settings atomically.
owner --> caller : Return a snapshot for the complete portable question.
caller -> handler : Offer that question and a short-lived native fallback.
handler -> owner : Optionally invoke the fallback under its captured guard.
owner --> handler : Return a status claim and any candidate coordinates.
handler -> reader : Recheck the returned point against every assumption.
note caller, reader : A saved record keeps the complete subject. A local callback does not travel with it.
#+end_sequence

There are two lifetimes. The outer execution callback ends with its request body. A handler's fallback ends as soon as that individual execution returns, even while the outer body is still open. Changing the live context or requested settings cannot silently change the frozen subject.

The [[file:evidence/retained-request/records-qualification.json][fresh-record receipt]] reopens the actual saved packets and durable ledger rows in two separate SDK-only processes, with formal solver exchanges and oracle methods denied. Wrong points stay invalid after re-encoding. Edited history stays recorded history. A proof requires its own checked object.

The current managed request profile admits Boolean, integer and exact rational real graphs. Wider theory profiles and their resource rules remain active work. This checkpoint strengthens term passing, state ownership and replay for both backends; it does not finish the formal-language goal.

Supporting evidence: [[file:evidence/retained-request/previous-readers-qualification.json][the prior-record compatibility receipt]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/retained-question.bl][the shared graph composer]] and [[file:sdk-src/formal/retained-composition.bl][the portable composition checker]].

Read [[file:RETAINED-REQUEST-IMPLEMENTATION.md][the interfaces and lifetime rules]], [[file:RETAINED-REQUEST-PLAN.md][the architectural review]], [[file:evidence/retained-request/qualification.json][the exact SDK qualification]] and [[file:TERM-FLOOR-MODULUS-MODEL.md][the separately checked arithmetic-to-term connection]].

** One number, one value
If two numbers are equal, equality should not change its mind when a third number enters the comparison. The earlier SDK broke that rule: =1.5M= equalled =1.5=, and =1.5= equalled =1.50M=, but the two Decimals differed. The selected numeric SDK repairs that finite-number relation on BEAM and JavaScript.

Scale still belongs to the stored value. Printing =1.50M= keeps those two decimal places. Code that needs to distinguish the representations asks =decimal/same-representation?= explicitly.

#+begin_rosetta
Question | Answer in this SDK | Why it matters
Are 1.5M, 1.5 and 1.50M equal by numeric value? | Yes, including the third side of the triangle. | Contracts can rely on a coherent numeric relation.
Are 1.5M and 1.50M the same representation? | No. Their coefficient and scale remain distinct. | Stored amounts keep their original scale.
Is a binary Float 0.1 exactly 0.1M? | No. The Float carries its actual binary64 value. | A printed approximation cannot silently become exact arithmetic.
Does equal mean interchangeable in every program? | Each operation needs its own outcome and effect rules. | Printing, scale inspection and Float/Decimal arithmetic still observe representation or kind.
Do ordinary maps and sets already use numeric value keys? | No. Their existing raw key relation remains separate. | Unifying language collection keys is the next semantic implementation.
#+end_rosetta

#+begin_graph :title "Meaning stays with Beam Lisp"
node value | Numeric value | Exact finite values | 0,0 | you
node scale | Representation | Coefficient and scale | 1,1 | station
node terms | Typed formal terms | Domain and failures | 1,0 | door
node engines | Z3 or OxiZ | Native solver claims | 2,0 | builder
node evidence | Rechecked evidence | Replay and trust | 3,0 | you
edge value -> scale | inspect scale | solid
edge value -> terms | define | solid
edge terms -> engines | ask | solid
edge engines -> evidence | return | solid
#+end_graph

** What changed in the measured comparisons
The same 30 finite values produce 900 ordered pairs on each route. The qualified numeric SDK has no discrepant pairs against the exact-value fixture. The earlier SDK had six on BEAM, fourteen in direct JavaScript and six through compiled BL. Those are measured fixture results, not estimates of all possible programs.

#+begin_waterfall :title "Direct JavaScript: the 14 discrepant pairs" :format count
start numeric_js_before | Discrepant pairs in the preceding SDK | signal
down numeric_js_fixed | Pairs now matching the exact-value fixture | green
total numeric_js_after | Discrepant pairs in the numeric SDK | green
#+end_waterfall

Click a figure to inspect its calculation and source. The observed counts are fixed; this chart does not let a slider rewrite a test result. The compiled BL route also executes all 1,051 existing captured Decimal oracle cases, including the named Decimal API and rounding operations. Values and raisedness agree in that corpus; complete exception and effect correspondence still needs its own work.

Two fresh SDK-only readers also reopen all 187 preceding managed graph records, including their saved numeric points, without a native exchange. The recorded producer SDK stays distinct from the current checking SDK. The saved native status remains a report; it does not become authenticated execution. See [[file:evidence/numeric-semantics/previous-readers-qualification.json][the actual compatibility and refusal checks]].

The modulus repair has a similar boundary. For a nonzero divisor, the translated result carries the divisor's sign: =(mod 7 -3)= is =-2=. Division by zero is a failed runtime outcome. Three separate native queries report the range, sign and quotient reconstruction laws. A separate Lean model checks the arithmetic formulas for arbitrary integers and keeps zero failure explicit. Its proof is about that mathematical model; the compiler and runtime connection remains reviewed rather than mechanically proved.

#+begin_honest :title "A firmer language foundation, with the remaining work visible"
These changes make ordinary BL values easier to specify. They do not make native UNSAT answers into certificates or turn every equality into a program rewrite. Collection key identity, literal evaluation order, wider Source admission and implementation correspondence remain active work. The retained original semantic canaries below keep their original SDK meaning.
#+end_honest

Read [[file:NUMERIC-IMPLEMENTATION.md][the numeric implementation and measured scope]], [[file:SEMANTICS.md][the semantic decisions]], [[file:evidence/numeric-semantics/qualification.json][the exact SDK and completed gates]], [[file:evidence/numeric-semantics/baseline/observation-summary.json][the original baseline]], [[file:evidence/numeric-semantics/candidate/observation-summary.json][the new three-route observations]], [[file:sdk-src/decimal.bl][the language Decimal API]], [[file:sdk-src/system/smt.bl][the legacy expression lowering]], [[file:metatheory/FloorModulus.lean][the integer model source]], [[file:FLOOR-MODULUS-MODEL.md][the mathematical modulus model]] and [[file:evidence/floor-modulus-model/qualification.json][its actual proof and axiom audits]].

** One portable request, either backend
A backend choice changes how we ask, not what we ask. The common request contains the original question graph, its replay policy and the requested native adapter. Both engines receive the same subject. BL checks their returned coordinates before using them as a witness.

The language now exposes this route through =formal.logic/prepare-request=, =execute-request=, =compare-request= and =recheck-request=. A request is ordinary portable data. Native session handles and the owner's private admission never enter it.

#+begin_sequence :title "A request can travel; execution stays local"
lanes app · one question | effect · optional handler | owner · native session | checker · fresh SDK scope | ledger · portable data
app -> effect : Pass the original request, policy and adapter choice.
effect -> owner : Delegate once through a local expiring fallback, or supply coordinates.
owner -> owner : Admit original terms and import shared nodes with one arena allocator.
owner --> checker : Return a graph-bound point and separately labelled native claims.
effect => checker : Freshly check the actual assertions before observations.
checker -> ledger : Store the request once and a separate answer body.
ledger --> checker : Reopen and recheck; saved labels grant no execution authority.
note owner, ledger : Source cursors and coordinates are data. Ports, callbacks and native IDs stay local.
#+end_sequence

Unfamiliar application keys stay data. Observation rows carry =label-source= and =descriptor-source= cursors into the original graph. Finding a term does not require rebuilding a host map or creating an atom from an input name. Every map value is visited in canonical graph-row order, including entries whose exact key kinds differ. Display materialization is a later, explicit step.

#+begin_rosetta :title "One interface, several useful answers"
Route | What the caller gets | What BL checks
Native Z3 or OxiZ | A reported status, original point graph and artifact labels | Fresh admission, coordinate encoding and actual assertion replay
Handler-supplied point | The same answer shape without opening a native owner | The same point checks; no caller verdict or trusted bit is accepted
Saved request | The original subject, policy, adapter and answer body | Exact reference binding, then a fresh point scope
Unchecked UNSAT | A solver claim with its reported conclusion | Format and subject binding; no certificate upgrade
#+end_rosetta

The [[#cell-story-formal-request][request example]] asks for =1/3= and =2/5= on both engines, then computes =2/15= in BL. It also checks a handler-supplied point, reopens a portable answer packet and rejects changed coordinates. The separate ledger qualification uses actual persisted records and two fresh SDK-only reader processes, with no application import path and no native exchanges.

#+begin_honest :title "A useful witness is not a compiler theorem"
The owner and reader share the SDK checker, running it in separate scopes. Replay is independent of the solver's claim and saved validation labels; it is not a second independently verified mathematical implementation. This first managed policy admits Bool/Int/Real sorts. Unsupported concrete interpretations remain incomplete. Native UNKNOWN and unchecked UNSAT remain visible. Full structured observations, retained-session language requests, wider value budgets and implementation/compiler/totality proofs remain open.
#+end_honest

Read the [[file:REQUEST-INTERFACE.md][public request guide]], [[file:MANAGED-GRAPH.md][owner transaction rules]], [[file:src/formal/question-forest.bl][compatible project delegate]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/request.bl][request format]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/request-runtime.bl][oracle bridge]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/request-point.bl][point reader]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/request-answer.bl][closed answer]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/graph-question.bl][raw-key inventory]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/coordinates.bl][coordinate reader]] and [[file:sdk-src/formal/request-record.bl][record integration]]. The [[file:evidence/managed-graph/qualification.json][core qualification]] and [[file:evidence/managed-integration/qualification.json][project integration receipt]] have separate scopes from this presentation.

The [[file:evidence/managed-graph/qualification.json][core qualification]] includes 15,853 targeted assertion executions, both 187-question native corpora, four fresh SDK-only readers and all 16 tamper controls. The numeric policy admits 94 of those questions; the other 93 refuse before a native scope call. Two fresh processes each recheck all saved records and replay the 52 SAT points, with zero native exchanges and no application import path. The persisted-ledger readers also leave unfamiliar key names absent.

The [[file:evidence/managed-integration/qualification.json][project integration]] adds 187 byte-identical inventory comparisons and 4,997 post-delegation assertion executions. The [[file:evidence/managed-integration/promotion.json][project now selects that exact SDK]]. Browser activation has a separate receipt.

#+begin_note :title "What the failed gates taught us"
The earlier [[file:evidence/managed-graph/candidate-2/candidate-review.json][declaration gate]] missed unused binder sorts. The [[file:evidence/managed-graph/candidate-3/candidate-review.json][fresh-reader gate]] then exposed application keys being decoded too early. Both repairs are in the selected SDK. Final qualification also caught a [[file:evidence/managed-graph/before-reader-control-dispatch/review.json][test-driver dispatch bug]]; the actual controls were rerun successfully. The superseded staged implementation has [[file:evidence/request-interface/source-archive.json][a source archive]] and a [[file:evidence/request-interface/request-preview-source-20261113.bundle][verified recovery bundle]]. Its earlier results keep their original scope.
#+end_note

The [[file:RETAINED-REQUEST-PLAN.md][next retained-request design]] puts every retained assumption inside the portable question before execution. A live session can then change how the question runs without silently strengthening its meaning. The present higher request bridge acquires fresh owners.


** A migration needs three separate answers
A message changes shape while a program is being upgraded. Before calling that change safe, we need three answers: does an old message fit the admitted input model, does the transformation keep the new promise, and can we show an input that breaks it?

Beam Lisp now keeps those questions as separate typed values. For a nonnegative integer message, adding one preserves the declared nonnegative result. Subtracting one thousand has a concrete counterexample. Both engines receive exactly the same questions; a separate BL checker checks the conditional proof and the supplied points.

#+begin_rosetta :title "What verified now means in the typed migration preview"
Evidence | Result | verified
Fresh conditional certificate and checked admitted input | Checked promise with a starting point | true
Checked original counterexample | The declared relation is broken | false
Native proved, portable search incomplete | Uncertified | nil
Conditional proof, admission not established | Checked conditional statement only | nil
Contradictory admission | Vacuous | nil
#+end_rosetta

Checkpoint 40 passes 9,891 assertion executions: 167 in four migration files, 299 preparation checks, 1,307 preceding focused checks and 8,118 regression checks. All 187 preceding paired questions retain their identities and verdicts. Three migration subjects are compared on both engines. Another process reopens six ledger records, including quoted Source functions, with zero managed query calls in the named rechecking window.

A transformation that raises has broken the normal-result promise. An undefined new admission predicate also fails the claim. Neither failure becomes an assumption that quietly removes the input. A separate equivalence mode requires the transformed message to keep the original successful value.

#+begin_code_graph :id formal-message-migration :title "Keep the model, evidence and actual call connected" :start model
node model | concept | Declare the old and new promises | observed
summary A closed definition names ordered Bool and Int inputs, the message role, the transformation and both admission predicates. The new contract can differ from the old one.
source priv/lib/formal/migration.bl | Closed migration definition | Builders produce portable data; their implementation is not the proof subject.
node questions | code | Rebuild the three actual questions | observed
summary Preparation reconstructs admitted inputs, conditional preservation and counterexample existence. Output completion stays in the claim.
source priv/lib/formal/migration.bl | Exact question reconstruction | Missing model inputs and changed sealed definitions are refused.
node certificate | constraint | Check the promise and its starting point | observed
summary The conditional certificate is checked afresh. Admission receives its own independently checked packet. Native proved cannot replace a missing certificate.
source priv/lib/formal/migration.bl | Separate certificate and point checkers | Verified true requires both checked facts; a missing fact leaves nil.
source priv/lib/formal/certificate.bl | Conditional term checking | Universal implementation soundness remains a separate boundary.
node counterexample | code | Retain an admitted failing input | observed
summary A negative result carries an independently checked original counterexample. Missing native coordinates are never invented.
source priv/lib/formal/witness.bl | Original assertion replay | False is a concrete value; unavailable is a different fact.
node source | code | Observe the stored Source function | observed
summary A quoted BL function is lowered afresh through compiler ANF. A supplied concrete point can execute that stored function and compare the outcome with term evaluation.
field Actual call | The observation retires its emitted module. It establishes one invocation, not universal compiler preservation.
source priv/lib/formal/source-runtime.bl | Owned concrete invocation | A compile or publication error remains an observer error.
source priv/std/reload/migrate.bl | Typed reload preview | A separate runtime function is neither invoked nor certified by the preview.
node ledger | view | Recheck after saving and reopening | observed
summary The existing ledger keeps complete definitions, questions and evidence. Another process reconstructs them and checks saved objects without managed query calls in the rechecking window.
source research/formal-logic/migration-check.bl | SDK-only exporter and fresh reader | Recording grants no mailbox delivery authority.
edge model -> questions | fixes the declared relation
edge questions -> certificate | separates implication from admission
edge questions -> counterexample | names the exact failing claim
edge model -> source | binds the stored Source
edge certificate -> ledger | retains checked conditional evidence
edge counterexample -> ledger | retains the original point
edge source -> ledger | keeps observation scope explicit
#+end_code_graph

Try [[#cell-story-message-migration][the same migration on both engines]]. Read [[file:MIGRATIONS.md][the interface and semantic decisions]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/migration.bl][the production term implementation]], [[file:migration-check.bl][the SDK-only program]], [[file:evidence/migrations/examples.json][the complete paired results]], [[file:evidence/migrations/ledger-read.json][the fresh reader]], [[file:evidence/migrations/focused-checks.txt][the migration checks]] and [[file:evidence/migrations/qualification.json][the exact qualification]].

#+begin_honest :title "A migration proof does not own a mailbox"
This previews a declared message relation. The first profile covers exact scalar Bool and Int inputs. A stored Source function has a reviewed ANF connection and can be observed at concrete points. Re-elaboration may execute phase code; it is not a sandbox. An unrelated runtime function has no such correspondence. Delivery, dispatch coverage and live generation coherence remain separate obligations. Every result keeps compiler and delivery authority false.
#+end_honest

** Time becomes a value you can reason about
An appointment lasts one tick and starts somewhere between zero and four. Does it intersect the window from two to three? Beam Lisp can now express the uncertain appointment as an ordinary value: shift a one-tick span by an endpoint with an input range.

Both engines find two concrete datings: one that intersects the window and one that misses it. The common language evaluator replays each dating against its complete question. That is what makes the answer /possible/. An engine that cannot decide has a different answer: /unknown/.

#+begin_rosetta
What we know | What the interface returns | What it carries
Every admitted dating intersects | certain | An intersecting witness and an unchecked UNSAT answer for separation.
Some datings intersect and some miss | possible | Independently replayed assignments for both questions.
Every admitted dating misses | impossible | A separated witness and an unchecked UNSAT answer for intersection.
The admitted conditions cannot hold | infeasible | Both native questions report UNSAT; their certificate status stays visible.
The engine cannot settle a question | unknown, candidate, unsupported or error | The unresolved answer and its diagnostics.
#+end_rosetta

#+begin_code_graph :id formal-temporal-values :title "One time value, two native engines, inspectable reasons" :start value
node value | code | Shift a span by an uncertain start | observed

node questions | code | Inspect both complete questions | observed
summary overlap-questions returns intersection and separation SAT questions in the same admitted context. Z3 and OxiZ receive the same question identities.
field Evidence | A positive possibility requires valid replay. Native UNSAT remains an unchecked answer.
source research/formal-logic/evidence/temporal/examples.json | Actual paired temporal questions | The report includes both concrete datings and selected SDK artifacts.

summary An empty span shares no points with another span. Every span covers an empty span. Empty spans at different coordinates have the same points and different endpoint data.

source research/formal-logic/TEMPORAL.md | Reviewed temporal semantics | Input range constraints and model admission remain separate fields of the value protocol.

summary Chronology questions use inclusive integer start ranges and strict-before edges. Namespaced keywords and symbols retain different variable identities and schedule keys.

source research/formal-logic/temporal-check.bl | Ordinary SDK consumer | Both engines replay the same forced chronology; selected Source calls are checked separately.

edge questions -> meaning | fixes the meaning of each answer

edge questions -> laws | supplies exact typed term statements
edge chronology -> boundary | identifies the declared coordinate model

#+end_code_graph

The preview review also fixes the concrete empty-span operations: overlap and disjointness agree, every span covers an empty span, union ignores an empty operand, and subtracting from an empty span yields no spans. Allen's thirteen-relation classification has a positive-width domain. Endpoint-data equality and equality of contained points have separate interfaces.

The temporal SDK passes 2,373 focused assertions and 7,086 formal assertions. All 187 preceding backend questions keep their identities and verdicts. The external proof-writer regression accepts 17 original-term proofs and rejects five altered originals. Three new temporal laws also have checked original-term Lean proofs; their constructor/runtime connection remains reviewed.

A whole masked decade and one unknown year in that decade also mean different things. The revised [[file:sdk-examples/datom/time/07-uncertain.bl][uncertainty example]] uses an endpoint range for the unknown year. The [[file:sdk-examples/datom/time/08-chronolog.bl][chronology example]] uses the same native term interface for both engines.

The running cells show [[#cell-story-temporal-values][both possibilities]], [[#cell-story-temporal-chronology][event identities and unresolved answers]], and [[#cell-story-temporal-laws][the original-term proof connections]].

** An answer has to fit its question
A solver response is useful only when its values and its meaning fit the question. Beam Lisp now checks that boundary in the SDK, above both engines.

The review found a concrete mistake: giving the number =1= to a Boolean variable could make the old replay accept a contradiction. Bool values now have to be =true= or =false=. The same rule extends to integer, rational, floating-point, enum, sequence and array models.

#+begin_rosetta
What arrived | What BL checks | What the caller sees
A Boolean variable assigned 1 | The binding has the wrong sort. | The proposed model is invalid.
A display saying 999 when the input is 7 | Replay computes the requested expression x + 1. | The standard observation is 8; the supplied display is retained separately.
SAT for only the assumptions | The proof goal still needs an answer. | The phase mismatch is refused.
An assumption loaded during delegation | The saved question context has changed. | The fallback refuses to answer under the changed context.
An unchanged native reply | Oracle matches it with its actual owned fallback result. | Native-only observations keep their provenance.
#+end_rosetta

#+begin_code_graph :id formal-answer-integrity :title "Keep the question, values and execution together" :start question
node question | code | Bind the whole question | observed
summary Reply binding preserves the exact formal-data identity of the question. Integer and float metadata, list and vector shapes, and floating bit patterns stay distinct.
source research/formal-logic/sdk-src/formal/answer.bl | Pure answer boundary | The reply cannot replace its question with a similar-looking value.
node bindings | code | Check the values' sorts | observed
summary A Boolean binding contains true or false. Concrete values for the other supported sorts have checked encodings. Missing values remain unavailable.
source research/formal-logic/sdk-src/formal/evaluate.bl | Typed independent replay | Malformed bindings cannot become a satisfying assignment through BL truthiness.
node observations | code | Rebuild what you display | observed
summary An ordinary handler's observations come from its assignments and the requested descriptors. Its supplied display is kept as reported data.
source research/formal-logic/src/formal/answer-examples.bl | Executable examples | Seven plus one displays eight even if the handler supplied 999.
node phase | code | Ask which question was answered | observed
summary Consistent assumptions and a proved goal are different facts. The phase and status must describe the complete requested question.
source research/formal-logic/sdk-src/formal/answer.bl | Phase and status rules | A frontend UNSAT claim cannot become an entailment.
node context | code | Keep delegation in its context | observed
summary Loading or resetting the native context advances its owner-held state. The owned fallback checks its original context before executing.
field Scope | Session maps are cooperative API handles. Raw native transport invalidates portable context accounting. Client bookkeeping is diagnostic; arbitrary Erlang introspection remains outside this boundary.
source research/formal-logic/sdk-src/formal/logic.bl | Context snapshot guard | Both native backends refuse the recorded context substitution.
node native | code | Preserve an exact native reply | observed
summary Oracle records its actual fallback result. Exact delegation retains native-only observations; a modified reply is independently reconstructed.
field Proof status | Native and handler answers keep unchecked certificate status. Compiler proof authority stays disabled.
source research/formal-logic/sdk-src/oracle.bl | Effect-owned execution record | Execution provenance is kept separate from proof checking.
edge question -> bindings | validate the proposed assignment
edge bindings -> observations | derive the requested values
edge question -> phase | check the response's meaning
edge question -> context | bind the tracked assumptions
edge context -> native | delegate the original question
edge native -> observations | retain matching provenance
#+end_code_graph

** The process keeps the real context
The process that owns the native port now also keeps the term registry and the assumptions actually loaded into it. The caller's atom is a diagnostic mirror. Erasing an entry there cannot erase an assumption from the question.

There is a second reason to keep a revision. A handler could load a new assumption and restore the old mirror before delegating. The old guard saw the same atom and continued. The owner now sees a different revision and refuses that execution.

#+begin_rosetta
What changes | What the owner does | What BL can report
The client forgets x = 7 | The owner retains the loaded assumption. | The complete question still names x = 7.
A handler restores the old client atom | The owner's revision has advanced. | The fallback stops before solving.
A raw native load adds false | Portable accounting becomes unavailable. | A typed question reports not-run until a managed reset.
A healthy raw context is reset | The owner uses its actual generation and starts a fresh registry. | Portable terms can be used again.
An unsupported exchange retires a worker | The completed result keeps its meaning; new execution is refused. | Unsupported stays unsupported.
#+end_rosetta

#+begin_code_graph :id formal-native-context-owner :title "One owner for the port and its meaning" :start journal
node journal | code | Keep the native journal | observed
summary The owner holds the portable term registry, retained assumptions, generation and a monotone context revision. It sends native IDs built from its own registry.
source research/formal-logic/sdk-src/formal/backend.bl | Managed owner state | The client mirror cannot replace the native registry or generation.
node question | code | Name the loaded assumptions | observed
summary The question layer reads the owner's snapshot. It adds the retained assumptions to the complete portable question, with their labels and digest.
field Portable data | The query carries no owner PID, port or native node IDs. The leased execution token stays outside the durable question.
source research/formal-logic/sdk-src/formal/logic.bl | Complete question construction | The handler still sees an assumption forgotten by the client.
node exchange | code | Check the revision before sending | observed
summary The owner checks the expected owner, generation and revision in the same actor turn as the native exchange. A stale token produces no native solver check.
source research/formal-logic/src/formal/native-context-examples.bl | Native check counters | A refused scope leaves the native check counter unchanged.
node mirror | code | Treat the atom as a mirror | observed
summary A caller can erase the local assumptions or restore an old snapshot. The owner retains its actual assumptions and revision.
source research/formal-logic/src/formal/native-context-examples.bl | Forgotten and restored mirrors | Both backends retain the question's context.
node raw | code | Mark raw accounting unavailable | observed
summary A raw operation other than hello cannot attest the original portable terms. The owner refuses further managed solving until a reset.
field Scope | This is API consistency. Arbitrary Erlang introspection and replacement of trusted runtime code are outside the boundary.
source research/formal-logic/sdk-src/formal/backend.bl | Raw compatibility transport | Native commands cannot silently alter a portable question.
node reset | code | Recover a healthy raw context | observed
summary A managed reset uses the owner's actual native generation. It clears assumptions and native IDs, restores portable accounting and advances the revision.
source research/formal-logic/sdk-src/formal/backend.bl | Managed reset | A failed native exchange still retires the worker and requires a new session.
node retirement | code | Preserve a completed refusal | observed
summary A native unsupported result can retire a worker without changing the question it answered. Post-execution identity matching keeps that result; it does not authorize another exchange.
field Proof status | Owner checks and replay remain implementation checks. Compiler proof authority stays disabled.
source research/formal-logic/sdk-src/formal/backend.bl | Identity and liveness | The first candidate exposed this distinction in the existing OxiZ sequence example.
edge journal -> question | retain the actual assumptions
edge question -> exchange | bind execution to its owner snapshot
edge mirror -> journal | read authoritative state
edge raw -> reset | reset before portable reuse
edge reset -> journal | start a fresh registry
edge exchange -> retirement | keep the completed result
edge raw -> exchange | refuse unknown portable accounting
#+end_code_graph

The focused SDK run passed 356 assertions: 136 owner checks and the preceding 220 typed-interface checks. The broader twenty-two-file run passed 7,086 assertions. All 187 previous native questions retain their identities and verdicts. The first candidate exposed one retirement projection bug and two fixture expectations; its failing record is retained.

Try [[#cell-story-native-contexts][the owner examples]] and [[#cell-story-native-context-revision][the native counter check]]. Read [[file:NATIVE-CONTEXTS.md][the context interface]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/backend.bl][the native owner]], [[file:src/formal/native-context-examples.bl][the examples]], [[file:native-contexts-check.bl][the recorder]], [[file:evidence/native-contexts/examples.json][both backends' recorded results]], [[file:evidence/native-contexts/baseline-checks.txt][the original failures]], [[file:evidence/native-contexts/candidate-1-focused-checks.txt][the first SDK qualification]], [[file:evidence/native-contexts/focused-checks.txt][the passing SDK checks]], [[file:evidence/native-contexts/regression-checks.txt][the regression]] and [[file:evidence/native-contexts/qualification.json][the qualification receipt]].

** Keep the handler with the work
A logic handler should still be there when a submitted task runs. The new implementation captures a handler table when Beam Lisp accepts managed work. A delayed child, a future, a registered callback and a generated actor keep that snapshot after the caller leaves its scope. The work retains the handler's executable code as well as its values.

#+begin_note :title "The submitted work owns its handler snapshot"
The selected immutable SDK passes 450 focused assertions, including 94 new handler and Source-boundary checks; 284 native lifetime/capacity assertions; and 7,086 formal regression assertions. All 187 preceding native questions retain their identities and verdicts. The compiler's B and C inventories agree byte for byte. The starting twenty process-boundary assertions passed six and failed fourteen on the preceding SDK.
#+end_note

#+begin_rosetta
When the boundary is crossed | Which handler should run? | Who keeps the code?
Managed spawn, future or callback submission | The table active at submission. | Each accepted task keeps its own code lease.
Nested handler extent | The selected effect's replacement table. | The extent retains its callbacks, then restores the preceding table.
Generated actor start | The start's snapshot, including after the caller returns. | Native start custody transfers the retained modules to the actor.
Explicit Source world entry | The entered world's context. | Source graph custody remains the authority for that world.
Fixed SDK framework adoption | Application tables are cleared. | The framework retains its fixed class rather than application callbacks.
Raw Erlang spawn | Its explicitly supplied native context. | It does not inherit the language's dynamic table automatically.
#+end_rosetta

#+begin_code_graph :id formal-effect-scopes :title "A handler has a scope and a lifetime" :start table
node table | code | Declare the methods | observed
summary effect/with-handler handles one named synchronous operation. effect/with-methods supplies a method table. An installed table missing a requested method refuses it; an entirely unhandled effect may run its default operation.
source research/formal-logic/sdk-src/effect.bl | Common language interface | Names are full keywords; arguments are eager BL values.
node capture | code | Snapshot accepted work | observed
summary Managed submission captures the registered callable context. It does not copy arbitrary process dictionary entries. A reused worker restores its own preceding table after invocation.
source priv/host/env.bl | Registered callable context | A copied context value alone grants no executable code custody.
node retain | code | Keep the callback alive | observed
summary The runtime retains direct handler modules and explicitly declared outside dependencies before scope entry and before publishing managed work. Source graph admission remains required.
field Captured code | Callbacks hidden inside arbitrary captured data need the existing explicit outside-dependency contract. Retaining a module grants no Source execution authority.
source test/bl/system/effect_test.bl | Scope and ownership checks | Bounds cover table count, method count, dependencies and snapshot bytes.
node oracle | code | Route a complete term question | observed
summary Oracle uses the same table for its script and query methods. Query answers still pass exact question binding and typed replay. Each child owns a fresh logical context rather than borrowing a native port.
source research/formal-logic/src/formal/effect-examples.bl | Delayed work examples | Both backend choices have deliberately unavailable native paths.
node world | code | Enter an explicit world | observed
summary Preparing a Source world clears the builder's callable tables. An explicit world entry uses that world's context. A Source entry can install a handler and submit its own managed work.
source priv/std/reload/generation.bl | Source world context | Ordinary calls preserve the active dynamic table.
node limits | code | Keep the claim precise | observed
summary These are synchronous scoped function calls. Continuation capture, effect typing, runtime preservation proofs and totality arguments remain separate work.
field Evidence | The recorded checks qualify these implementation cases. Native and handler answers keep their existing certificate status. Compiler proof authority stays disabled.
source research/formal-logic/EFFECTS.md | Interface and qualification scope | No handler table is portable proof data.
edge table -> capture | capture at submission
edge capture -> retain | retain before accepting work
edge retain -> oracle | invoke under the owned snapshot
edge world -> capture | submit inside the selected world
edge oracle -> limits | validate the answer independently
#+end_code_graph

The [[#cell-story-managed-effects][managed-work cell]] runs the same handled question through four kinds of work for each backend choice. Native paths are deliberately unavailable. Every answer contains seven and passes typed replay; its certificate stays unchecked. The [[#cell-story-named-effects][named-effect cell]] shows ordinary default delegation and restoration. A separate SDK test confirms that conveying a handler cannot transfer its captured native session to a child.

Read [[file:EFFECTS.md][the interface and lifetime rules]], [[file:sdk-src/effect.bl][the shared language module]], [[file:sdk-src/oracle.bl][Oracle's integration]], [[file:src/formal/effect-examples.bl][the executable examples]], [[file:evidence/effects/examples.json][their actual results]], [[file:evidence/effects/focused-checks.txt][the focused checks]], [[file:evidence/effects/runtime-checks.txt][native lifetime checks]], [[file:evidence/effects/capacity-checks.txt][the separate capacity check]], [[file:evidence/effects/regression-checks.txt][the formal regression]] and [[file:evidence/effects/qualification.json][the qualified SDK and source receipt]].

** Equality follows the value's sort
Two floating-point bit patterns can represent the same NaN value in the SMT model. That equality must keep working inside a sequence, as an array's default value and when the value becomes an array key. The independent replay now follows those rules recursively.

#+begin_rosetta
The distinction | The meaning
Formal data identity | The written representations retain different data identities.
SMT floating-point identity | Each format has one logical NaN value, so the two representations name the same array key.
IEEE comparison | fp.eq has its own comparison rule, including false when a NaN is involved.
Backend availability | Z3 answers all three new ground questions. OxiZ answers the two array questions and reports the sequence question unsupported.
#+end_rosetta

These are direct SMT-model questions. A program that needs to preserve a NaN payload can use an explicit bit-vector representation. The [[#cell-story-nested-equality][executable comparison]] keeps both native verdicts visible.

The new SDK file passed 134 assertions, and the preceding 86 SDK assertions still pass. The broader run passed 7,086 assertions across twenty-two files. All 187 previous native questions retain their identities and verdicts.

#+begin_note :title "A stronger interface, with its evidence basis intact"
Typed replay, native execution and certificate checking remain separate reasons to trust an answer. This slice fixes concrete interface failures and checks the recorded cases. General implementation soundness and compiler preservation remain work ahead. The handler checkpoint above adds managed process inheritance with explicit code custody.
#+end_note

Try [[#cell-story-answer-integrity][the handled-answer example]] and [[#cell-story-answer-context][the tracked-context example]]. Read [[file:ANSWER-INTEGRITY.md][the interface and semantic choices]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/answer.bl][the answer boundary]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/evaluate.bl][typed replay]], [[file:src/formal/answer-examples.bl][the executable examples]], [[file:answers-check.bl][the example recorder]], [[file:evidence/answer-integrity/examples.json][the retained examples]], [[file:evidence/answer-integrity/baseline-checks.txt][the original failing baseline]], [[file:evidence/answer-integrity/focused-checks.txt][the 220 SDK checks]], [[file:evidence/answer-integrity/regression-checks.txt][the wider regression]] and [[file:evidence/answer-integrity/qualification.json][the qualification receipt]].

** The same value, after it moves
A saved execution record should keep its identity in another VM. The first context archive did not: its definition and profile were intact, but the receipt hash changed after transfer. The implementation had mistaken default ETF bytes for a canonical data encoding.

There is now one versioned identity codec for the formal objects. It names public data contents rather than a VM's map order or vector storage layout. Full keyword namespaces and data kinds stay distinct. Closed objects carry an explicit identity profile, and the checker reconstructs their digest.

#+begin_rosetta
The question | The field or boundary | What it says
What does this program mean? | Semantic profile | The value, error and binding rules in its claim.
How do we name this data? | Identity profile | A versioned canonical encoding; the name survives the qualified transfer.
Which engine tried the question? | Backend provenance | The actual Z3 or OxiZ artifact and result.
Which code may execute? | Runtime custody | Owned code and resource admission; a hash grants no authority.
#+end_rosetta

All nine newly recorded executions now recheck in a fresh VM: eight conform and the ordinary program's recorded disagreement stays a disagreement. The two context-backed function certificates and the source-protocol proof also recheck. The calibrated window records no handler calls or loads of the observed handler modules.

The coherent 22-file run passed 7,099 assertions, including 50 identity checks and eight independent byte/hash fixtures. The original 187 backend questions retain their expected results. Five original BEAM artifacts still reproduce byte for byte, while their old and new core identities are reported separately.

#+begin_note :title "An intentional experimental format change"
Earlier raw-ETF objects remain evidence for their recorded checker versions. They are preserved with the failing transfer logs. The new checker requires the canonical identity profile; it does not rewrite historical executions or silently promote old proof hashes into the new format.
#+end_note

Read [[file:DATA-IDENTITY.md][the identity format and version boundary]], [[file:sdk-src/formal/identity.bl][the shared data codec]], [[file:test/identity_test.bl][the kind, namespace and transfer checks]], [[file:evidence/contexts/canonical-fixtures.json][the independent byte fixtures]], [[file:evidence/contexts/original-byte-reproduction.json][the old BEAM byte comparison]], and the [[file:evidence/contexts/fresh-replay.json][fresh recorded-data and proof checks]].

* Try the language ideas
A total solver array has an entry at every index. A BL map can have no entry at all. That small difference is why the language layer matters.

#+begin_rosetta
Question | What BL needs to describe | What we can ask
Did I find a key? | Presence independently of the stored value | Show that Some nil and None differ.
Can I append this value? | Successful evaluation independently of model capacity | A full bounded model may need more room even when append is a valid language operation.
Can this account go negative? | State, a guard and the next state | Find a short overdraft trace or prove the modeled guard preserves the balance.
Does the codec round-trip? | Field widths, byte order and overflow policy | Prove unpack(pack(fields)) returns the fields in the declared layout.
#+end_rosetta

* What the experiment tells us
The comparison uses identical questions and language meanings. An undecided answer stays visible. A SAT result is called a candidate when our independent replay does not cover its domain yet.

#+begin_rosetta
Recorded corpus | Questions | Z3 interpreted expected answers | OxiZ interpreted expected answers
Language values and state models | 61 | 61 | 56
Native theories and boundaries | 95 | 88 | 55
Bit layouts and declared floating-point outcomes | 31 | 31 | 17
Total | 187 | 180 | 128
#+end_rosetta

#+begin_matrix
Capability in this prototype | Z3 | OxiZ
The same typed BL question | y | y
Finite maps, sets and bags | y Scoped laws retained | y Scoped laws retained
Bit layouts and byte-order laws | y | y
Declared floating-point outcomes | y Tested binary16 cases | ~ Many unknown results
Native sequences | y Tested fragment | n Adapter boundary
Checked native solver proof certificates | n Not checked | n Not checked
Default compiler cutover | n Future work | n Future work
#+end_matrix

OxiZ's remaining outcomes are 36 unknown, 18 unsupported and five candidates. Z3 has five candidates and two explicit adapter boundaries. The corrected corpus has no opposing decisive answers and no invalid replayed models. These counts describe this experiment, not every problem either solver can handle.

#+begin_note :title "What OxiZ contributes"
OxiZ gives us a Rust solver implementation to inspect and extend beside the Rust worker. That is useful research territory. Typed terms, language-aware maps and symbolic traces are BL interface work that benefits both backends. Z3 also has native AST and user-propagator APIs.
#+end_note

#+begin_warning :title "A bug the comparison caught"
The first direct OxiZ route treated two NaN bit encodings as different SMT values. Independent replay rejected its answer. The adapter now normalizes NaN literals to the one NaN value in SMT floating-point semantics. The original failing run is retained. General symbolic floating-point completeness remains unqualified.
#+end_warning

* Better language decisions
Preview gives us room to choose coherent rules. The goal is a language programmers can reason about without discovering exceptions by accident.

#+begin_decision :id story-equality :title "Make numeric equality an equivalence relation" :status proposed
- [x] Compare finite numbers by exact value; give Decimal representation equality an explicit name.
- [ ] Preserve the scale-sensitive Decimal exception in generic equality.

The earlier SDK returned true, true, false for the equality bridge between 1.5M, 1.5 and 1.50M. The selected numeric SDK repairs that finite-number relation on BEAM and JavaScript. The original [[file:proposals/numeric-equality.patch][BEAM]] and [[file:proposals/numeric-equality-js.patch][JS companion]] diffs remain as review history; the qualified source also adds the named Decimal JS API. The [[file:SEMANTICS.md][semantic review]] keeps exact numeric value, Decimal representation and collection key identity distinct. See the new numeric chapter for the actual gates and remaining collection work.
#+end_decision

#+begin_note :title "The same numeric inputs on both targets"
The next semantic batch has [[file:proposals/finite-number-cases.json][30 shared exact values and 900 ordered pairs]], with prepared [[file:semantic-number-corpus.bl][BEAM]] and [[file:semantic-number-corpus.mjs][JS runtime]] observers. Float bits, Decimal scale and large integers survive the input format. The probes have not run yet. They will compare numeric and nested equality while recording collection-key behavior separately. The [[file:proposals/modulus-lowering.patch][modulus repair]] also remains unapplied: its proposed laws explicitly require a nonzero divisor.
#+end_note

#+begin_decision :id story-presence :title "Give lookup and strict access clear contracts" :status proposed
- [x] Keep missing separate from present nil; expose strict access and defaulting lookup as named operations.
- [ ] Let one ambiguous operation quietly switch between absence, nil and failure.

The prototypes already model these relations separately. Which short names BL should use is still a language-design choice.
#+end_decision

* Could Beam Lisp be fully formal?
Yes, if we choose a precise promise and build toward it in layers. There are several different ambitions hiding in that phrase.

Fully formal and total are different ideas. A formal semantics can describe divergence, exceptions, nondeterminism and long-running processes. A total core is an optional foundation choice for computations and proof terms that must terminate.

#+begin_rosetta
Ambition | What it would mean for BL | What it does not buy automatically
Precise semantics | Every core construct has a mathematical account of values, outcomes and effects. | A proof that every program meets its author's intent.
Proof-aware programs | A module can carry contracts, assumptions and checked evidence. | A decision procedure for every possible property.
A total core | Core functions terminate; recursion needs a structural argument or another checked termination proof. | A ban on long-running actors in the effectful language around it.
A verified compiler | Compilation preserves the specified source behavior under an explicit target/runtime model. | Verification of OTP, the OS, hardware and every foreign library.
A fully verified application | Its stated end-to-end properties follow from checked component proofs and explicit environment assumptions. | Proof that its requirements are complete or the real world obeys those assumptions.
#+end_rosetta

** The direction I would choose
A small pure core, a rich effectful language around it, and evidence that travels with the code. Keep ordinary programming pleasant. Make a stronger claim require a stronger, visible justification.

#+begin_principles
Specify the core | Values, equality, evaluation order, exceptions and the meaning of each numeric/text operation belong in a precise semantics.
Distinguish partial correctness and termination | “If this returns, its balance is nonnegative” differs from “this always returns.” Total correctness needs both.
Keep effects explicit | Host calls, state, actors, time and failures need contracts and trace semantics. A snapshot becomes a stated assumption.
Make proof search replaceable | Solvers and tactics can propose evidence. A small checker validates supported proof objects against the exact claim.
Carry the dependency ledger | Report the definitions, assumptions, axioms, profiles, artifacts and runtime contracts each theorem uses.
Preserve meaning through compilation | Prove a lowering pass or validate each translation; retain the source-to-ANF-to-target connection.
#+end_principles

#+begin_graph :title "A proposed foundation for a formal BL"
node source | BL source | Ordinary programs and contracts | 0,0 | you
node core | Specified core | Typed, explicit evaluation and outcomes | 1,0 | station
node obligation | Obligations | Preconditions, effects and termination | 2,0 | door
node search | Search engines | Z3, OxiZ, induction and tactics | 2,1 | builder
node proof | Proof objects | Bound to the exact claim and assumptions | 3,1 | station
node checker | Small checker | The trusted proof rules | 3,0 | door
node compiler | Checked compilation | Preserved or validated behavior | 4,0 | builder
edge source -> core | elaborate | solid
edge core -> obligation | generate | solid
edge obligation -> search | ask | dash
edge search -> proof | propose | dash
edge proof -> checker | validate | solid
edge checker -> compiler | checked evidence | solid
edge core -> compiler | specified meaning | solid
#+end_graph

The small checker now has a first executable fragment: Boolean case splits and linear integer contradiction certificates. Our native UNSAT answers still carry unchecked solver certificates; the new proof producer constructs a separate proof object rather than trusting that answer. Independent SAT replay checks concrete witnesses; it does not validate an UNSAT proof. Extending proof production and checking to the other theories remains a substantial project.

** Can it still feel like a Lisp?
Yes. The surface language can keep macros, interactive evaluation and convenient data structures. The formal boundary would sit underneath, after names and macros have been resolved into a small core.

A macro can generate code and proof obligations without becoming trusted. A tactic can search for an argument without becoming trusted. The checker judges the resulting core term or proof object. Arbitrary runtime eval and unmodeled host calls still need an explicit boundary.

Dynamic values can enter a checked module through a predicate or checked conversion. A theorem may then say “for every input admitted by this contract.” It should not silently turn Any into a precisely typed value. A staged or gradual adoption path could keep ordinary BL useful while selected modules make stronger promises.

#+begin_rosetta
What a programmer writes | What the formal boundary should establish
A function with a result contract | The implementation satisfies that contract under its stated preconditions.
A recursive definition in the total core | Its recursive calls decrease in a checked well-founded order.
A long-running actor | Its transition function and permitted traces satisfy the declared safety rules; progress has separate assumptions.
A macro or proof tactic | Its generated output passes the same checks as handwritten output.
A host call | Its trusted contract or runtime check is visible in the dependency ledger.
#+end_rosetta

** What should count as a proof?
The first practical target could be a narrow certificate system for a useful fragment. Equality and linear integer reasoning are possible starting points; exact proof rules, integer cuts and normalization would need careful specification. A general SMT proof can contain theory lemmas, so checking only a propositional SAT certificate would leave part of the argument unchecked.

There are several viable foundation choices: a logical framework with explicit inference rules, a dependent type theory, or an external proof assistant that checks exported statements. We should choose one small trusted route before committing to a large proof language. The present portable term layer can feed any of these; it does not force BL to copy Lean's surface syntax.

A checked proof should name the exact claim, semantic profile, definitions and assumptions. Its validity should follow from those meanings and proof rules. The solver build that discovered it remains useful provenance; it need not become a logical premise once an independent checker validates the full proof.

#+begin_decision :id formal-kernel-strategy :title "Start with a small checked fragment, then grow" :status proposed
- [x] Pick one proof system and one useful fragment, with an explicit assumption ledger and independently checked proof objects.
- [ ] Build a large new proof assistant before testing a useful end-to-end contract.
- [ ] Label every solver UNSAT result as a checked theorem.

A possible first slice is an integer contract through resolved ANF, with a checkable argument for a narrow class of obligations. It must demonstrate both the proof rules and the source-to-statement connection.
#+end_decision

** How a proof could outlive an engine
The timeline below is a design example. It assumes the future checker exists, the claim and assumptions keep the same meaning, and the proof rules remain supported. Its months are illustrative, not delivery commitments.

A solver upgrade could change which proofs we discover and how quickly. A fully checked proof would stand on its statement and proof rules, while retaining the old solver build as provenance. Today's unchecked solver answers do not yet have that independence.

#+begin_lifetime :id formal-proof-lifetime :title "Stable meaning, replaceable proof search" :start 2026-11 :end 2027-06
lane meaning | The stated promise | now
lane checker | Checked evidence | next
lane search | Proof search | later
span meaning | 2026-11 | 2027-06 | Same meaning and assumptions
span search | 2026-11 | 2027-02 | First search engine
handoff search -> checker | 2027-01 | Candidate checked
span checker | 2027-01 | 2027-06 | The checked proof remains valid
span search | 2027-03 | 2027-06 | Replacement engine
marker 2027-03 | Solver upgrade
#+end_lifetime

** The compiler and the checker also need a trust story
Self-hosting a checker in BL does not verify the BL compiler. If the compiler or runtime misexecutes that checker, the logical boundary can fail. We should record that dependency, use an independently implemented checker, or verify/validate the relevant compilation path. Bootstrapping and packaging are part of the architecture.

For the compiler, two routes can complement each other. We can prove selected lowering or optimization passes once, or validate individual translations against their input. Both need the intended observation relation: successful values, exceptions, effects and traces. Equality of two values alone may be too weak to justify substituting one representation for another in every program context.

#+begin_honest :title "A proof still depends on a statement people can understand"
The strongest result is useful only when the contract describes the behavior we intended. Keep the exact statement, examples, counterexamples, assumptions and trust dependencies readable beside the code. Formal foundations should make those conversations easier.
#+end_honest

** What we would have to decide
#+begin_schema :id formal-foundation-model :title "The evidence model we would want"
claim @file | A precise statement, tied to a semantic profile.
  subject text | Stable identity of the term graph and definitions.
  profile text | Language meaning and observation relation.
  assumptions ->> assumption | The conditions under which this is claimed.
  proof -> proof | Evidence for this exact statement.
assumption @link | An explicit dependency, not an invisible convenience.
  kind axiom, runtime-contract, input-condition | Why it is admitted.
  statement text | The precise condition.
proof @seal | A proposed proof object.
  rules text | Identifies the proof system and its version.
  checked-by -> checker | The checker that accepted it.
checker @eye | The small implementation we trust for this proof system.
  artifact text | Exact implementation identity.
  theories text | Supported proof rules, not advertised solver modules.
#+end_schema

This is a conceptual model for the wider foundation. The first certificate checker already binds its evidence to an exact statement and lists assumptions and implementation trust. The diagram does not install a database schema or imply that the full foundation is implemented.

#+begin_code_graph :id formal-trust-path :title "Where trust must be earned" :start semantics
node semantics | code | Language semantics | proposed
summary Define equality, evaluation, outcomes and effects precisely.
source research/formal-logic/SEMANTICS.md | semantic review | Records concrete bugs and design choices.
node lowering | code | Compiler connection | partial
summary The prototype lowers a reviewed scalar ANF fragment into terms.
rule Refuse unreviewed calls and unsupported runtime profiles.
source research/formal-logic/sdk-src/formal/source.bl | formal.source/prepare | Current scoped lowering, not a verified compiler.
node solver | code | Solver results | implemented
summary Both native adapters attempt the same typed question.
source research/formal-logic/sdk-src/formal/logic.bl | formal.logic/solve-in | Keeps solver status, replay and authority separate.
node kernel | code | Proof checker | partial
summary The first Boolean/linear Int certificate checker recomputes every branch and weighted contradiction.
source research/formal-logic/sdk-src/formal/certificate.bl | formal.certificate/check | 511 focused certificate assertions; implementation soundness is not mechanically proved.
rule Backend agreement cannot replace this check.
node runtime | constraint | OTP and foreign code | proposed
summary State which behavior is modeled and which runtime contracts are assumed.
rule A theorem about a pure core does not prove a live process is healthy.
edge semantics -> lowering | defines the behavior to preserve
edge lowering -> solver | produces obligations
edge solver -> kernel | should supply checkable evidence
edge runtime -> semantics | defines the effect boundary
#+end_code_graph

** The important limits
General recursion makes automatic termination and arbitrary program correctness undecidable in general. We can choose decidable fragments, accept user-supplied invariants and termination arguments, or return unknown. “Formal” does not mean the machine can answer every question. See [[https://www.cs.virginia.edu/~robins/Turing_Paper_1936.pdf][Turing's original computability result]] and [[https://lean-lang.org/doc/reference/latest/Definitions/Recursive-Definitions/][Lean's explicit treatment of recursion and termination]].

Actors can run indefinitely by design. We can prove safety (“no invalid message is sent”) while leaving progress conditional on scheduler fairness, delivery and failure assumptions. Cancellation, timeouts and hot upgrades need their own observable trace semantics.

Foreign calls should expose explicit contracts and admission policies. Treating an unmodeled host function as a pure mathematical function would hide exactly the uncertainty the interface is meant to reveal.

A checked proof can still establish the wrong specification. We need understandable contracts, executable examples and witnesses to help people review whether the claim is the one they intended. A typed witness now lets us show that a set of preconditions actually has an example, alongside the separate proof of what follows from those preconditions.

The [[#prove-the-promise-on-the-core-program][checked Core contract]] makes one part of that design concrete. For every admitted input, a must-return program in the finite Core fragment yields a value that meets its contract. Named failures can be tracked explicitly by a different policy. This gives us a place to attach a checked decrease argument when the fragment grows to include loops.

#+begin_honest :title "A total core inside an effectful language"
I recommend exploring a total, pure core with explicit effects around it. Keep a supported general-programming path for actors and interop, with its assumptions visible. That gives BL a strong foundation without pretending the entire BEAM and outside world are already verified.
#+end_honest

** What this could feel like
#+begin_quote
“This refactor preserves the result, the failure behavior and the declared effects. Here are the assumptions it uses. This module still relies on an unverified host contract.”
#+end_quote

That is a useful language feature: a concrete, inspectable statement attached to the program. A proposed declaration might combine a precondition, a result contract, an effect signature and a termination claim. The exact surface syntax should follow the semantics, rather than deciding the semantics accidentally.

#+begin_decision :id formal-total-core-direction :title "Explore a total pure core and explicit effects" :status proposed
- [x] Specify a small core, allow stronger contracts and proofs, and make effect/runtime assumptions explicit.
- [ ] Require every ordinary BL program to carry a total-correctness proof immediately.
- [ ] Treat an SMT verdict alone as a complete formal foundation.

This is a recommended research direction. It does not change BL's language semantics or activate a new compiler gate.
#+end_decision

Primary reference points: [[https://lean-lang.org/doc/reference/latest/Elaboration-and-Compilation/][Lean separates elaboration from a small trusted kernel]], [[https://lean-lang.org/doc/reference/latest/Axioms/][Lean exposes axioms and additional trust dependencies]], and [[https://compcert.org/man/manual001.html][CompCert states an explicit behavior-preservation guarantee]]. These illustrate architectural principles, not proofs about BL. The design here is our proposed application of those principles.

* A path we can actually build
#+begin_milestones :id formal-delivery :title "From useful terms to a formal foundation" :start M1
milestone M0 | done | A common term interface | Foundation
check The retained 187-question corpus and 795 assertions describe the implemented prototype.
milestone M1 | active | Choose coherent language rules | Language design
needs M0
step Resolve equality, key identity, outcomes and the numeric/text profiles.
check Review current behavior, proposed changes and affected consumers.
gate Language decisions remain open for review.
milestone M2 | active | Specify the pure core | Formal foundation
needs M1
step Int/Bool core, reference and symbolic interpretations, exact errors and reviewed ANF elaboration are implemented.
check Prove basic metatheory for the chosen core and make trust dependencies explicit.
milestone M3 | active | Check proof objects | Formal foundation
needs M2
step Original Int/Bool entailment and concrete SAT existence have independent checkers. The exact finite Core contract composes both. Extend the same account to translations, loop obligations and wider effects.
check Reject malformed proofs and proofs for the wrong claim or assumptions.
milestone M4 | active | Connect compilation | Compiler
needs M2 M3
step Prove selected passes or validate their translations.
check Qualify meaning preservation against the specified core and target boundary.
milestone M5 | active | Extend actors and interop contracts | Runtime
needs M4
step Source-bound traces and a sequential owned BEAM observer are implemented; extend effects and environment semantics.
check Keep finite conformance, universal refinement, bounded exploration and liveness claims distinct.
#+end_milestones

#+begin_roadmap :id story-roadmap :title "What is useful at each stage"
now | Inspect a formal question | Terms, language models and retained results already work. | M0
next | Fix the semantics we can explain | Qualify coherent equality and the old modulus correction before production migration. | M1
later | Make proofs travel with compiled code | A specified core, proof objects and checked lowering build a stronger foundation. | M2–M5
#+end_roadmap

#+end_procedure
